Secretary of State.
PEPOT MENT OF STATE
My dear Mr. Secretary:
There are enclosed copies of correspondence from Mr. F. W.
Mathias of the Pacific Coast Oyster Growers Association of
Olympia, Washington, dated October 12, which has been received by the
Director of the Fish and Wildlife Service of this Department.
This correspondence requests assistance in obtaining from Japan
seed oysters for transplantation in suitable waters of the Pacific
Coast in order to insure a continuance of oyster production in that
region. An officer of the Fish and Wildlife Service has been in-
formed verbally by an officer of the Commodities Division of the
1117
Department of State that the Secretary of State has received a let-
ter from the National Canners Asscciation requesting aid in arrang-
ing for export to the United States of Japanese seed oysters.
Japanese oysters were introduced on the Pacific Coast a number
of years ago and the annual imports of seed from Japan, which con-
timued until the declaration of war by Japan, resulted in sufficient
production of adult oysters to support a sizable oyster industry on
the Pacific Coast.
The native oyster of the Pacific Coast, the Olympia oyster, is
very small and attempts were made to introduce the larger Atlantic
oysters. Pacific water temperatures were too low for successful
spawning of introduced Atlantic Coast oysters. Japanese seed oysters
were then imported. The Japanese oysters do spawn in certain areas
of the Pacific Coast, although temperature and other conditions are
unfavorable in certain seasons.
It is believed very desirable that some arrangement be made
whereby Pacific Coast oyster growers can again obtain seed oysters
from Japan. The Fish and Wildlife Service, however, believes that
the export of Japanese seed oysters to the United States should not be
handled through a single exporter who held a virtual monopoly, as in
IAN 2OD
the past. This monopoly operated very much to the disadvantage of
Pacific Coast oyster growers.

=== Page 2 === (confidence 1.00)
It is recommended that the Department of State take such
action as it believes appropriate to arrange for export of Japanese
seed oysters to the Pacific Coast of the United States, if such
action would be consistent with the economic policies now being
pursued by the military government of Japan.
Sincerely yours,
Assistant Secretary.
Enclosure 17.

PACIFIC COAST OYSTER.GROWERS ASSOCIATION
PHONE 6321
202 FIRST NATIONAL BANK BUILDING
OLYMPIA, WASHIN GTON
October 12, 1945
Mr. Ira N. Gabrielson, Director
Fish and Wild Life Service
Department of the Interior
Washington, D. C.
Dear Mr. Gabrielson:
I am enclosing copy of a letter sent to Allied
Supreme Commander, General MacArthur in Toyko. This letter
was authorized at the last meeting of the Pacific Coast
Oyster Crowers Association at Raymond.
At the meeting, it was stated that M. Yamishita,
Japanese who formerly imported seed and furnish it on con-
signment basis to Japanese growers in this country, is again
attempting to get into the seed oyster business. He has
been approaching oyster growers in Washington, Oregon and
California to place orders with him for seed oysters.
The association is definitely against Mr. Yamishita
having anything to do with the seed oyster business.
We will be very glad to have any assistance you
can give in the matter of obtaining seed oysters.
The Pacific oyster set in Willipa and Grays Harbors,
Hood Canal and Puget Sound waters has been almost nil in
the past two years.
With kind regards.
Sincerely,
(Sgd.) F. W. MATHIAS
F. W. Mathias, Director and
Seed Committee Chairman

=== Page 4 === (confidence 0.98)
Q.
October 2, 1945
General Douglas MacArthur
Allied Supreme Commander
Toyko, Japan
Dear General MacArthur:
We will val ue your aid in obtaining information for
the guidance of this association in obtaining seed oysters from
Japan.
Large quantities of seed oysters, species - Cstrea Gegas
raised in Japanese waters have been shipped to oyster growers in
Washington, Oregon and California for a period of fifteen to twenty
years. This seed oyster importation business, which started in the
1920's, continued through the 1930's and ended with the Japanese
attack on Pearl Harbor.
The "Pacific" oyster industry developed rapidly in Puget
Sound, Willipa and Grays Harbors, Washington and extended to the
Oregon coastal harbors and to northern California harbors.
The Pacific Coast Oyster rowers Associetion was organized
under requirements of the National Recovery Act and also for the
protection of the oyster growers in the three Pacific Coast states.
Many problems were encountered that included an attempt by Japanese
to control the oyster industry in Washington, Oregon and Califomia.
This association was able to protect the interests of the
American Oyster Crowers and acted as the sole seed oyster importing
organization, following many troublesome encounters with Japanese
interests.
The Japanese government, represented by the Japanese
Caune
Counsel in Seattle, finally worked out a program, enabling this
association to deal with the seed oyster growers in the Sendai Bay
region on the Island of Honshu. The seed oysters from this area
gave satisfactory results and were inspected by Japancse govern-
ment biologists, certifying that no drills of other detrimental
marine life was being shipped with the boxes of seed oysters.
It is the wish of this association to deal direct with
the oysters growers of the Sendai Bay area, obtaining the best
quality of seed oysters that will pass inspection of Japanese and
United States biologists.
We would like to know (1) when contact may be made with
the growers or an association of growers of seed oysters in Sendai

=== Page 5 === (confidence 0.98)
. C. Q P I.
General Douglas MacArthur
Page 2
October 2, 1945
Bay area; (2) Was there an oyster seed catch in 1945; (3) Extent
of seed catch; (4) When business relations can be re-opened with
the Sendai Bay seed growers; (5) Officer or Federal Department
to contact for negotiations.
We will appreciate your aid in obtaining the dosired
information.
Faithfully,
F. W. Mathias, Director and
Chairman, Seed Oyster Committee

ENCLOSURE
POLICY WITH RESPECT TO FISHING AND AQUATIC
INDUSTRIES
IN
JAPAN
Report by the
State-War-Navy Coordinating Subcommittee for the Far East
THE PROBLEM
1. To determine United States policy during the occupation
period in regard to the Japanese fishing and aquatic industries.
FACTS BEARING
ON
THE
PROBLEM
2. pee Appendix "A".
DIDCUSDION
3. see Appendix "B".
CONCLUSIONS
4. During the period of occupation, the Supreme Commander
should be guided, subject to military considerations, by the
following general prineiples:
2. In order to meet domestic consumption requirements,
the supreme Commander should:
(1) Insist that appropriate available vessels,
facilities, gear, equipment and supplies in Japanese
hands be put to use;
(2) Take such steps as he may deem practical and
necessary to provide suffic ent fuel for allocation
to I shing boats;
(3) Require the Japanese Government to rehabilitate
the production facilities of the fishing, fish ferti-
lizer and seafood processing industries, and facilities
for distribution of their products; and
(4) Furnish such other assistance, subject to
general policies governing aid to Japanese industry,
as ne deems necessary.

=== Page 11 === (confidence 0.99)
b. The coastal fisheries and fish culture should be
utilized as the primary sources for domestic consumption.
To the extent that fish culture and coastal fisheries are
unable to meet the minimum domestic requirements, deep
sea fisheries and other fisheries in water open to Japanese
operation may be utilized where security and political
considerations permit. Deep sea fishing in areas near
United States territory or near United States island
responsibilities should not be authorized. Japanese fish-
ing should not be permitted near areas under Allied juris-
diction without prior permission from the country concerned.
These prohibitions should continue until international
agreenents are negotiated permitting Japanese fishing in
these areas.
c. In order to determine (1) the effect on Japanese
food supply of restrictive measures enforced for security
or other reasons, and (2) the extent to which the United
States and other nations should be permitted to exploit
fisheries previously exploited by the Japanese, the
Supreme Commander should immediately obtain from the
Japanese Government available surveys and other data con-
cerning the resources of all Pacific fishing areas
previously exploited by the Japanese.
d. Japanese fishing operations should conform strictly
to:
(1) The provisions of agreements relating to whaling
to which the United States is a party;
(2) The provisions of other agreements relating to
conservation to which the United States is a party;
(3) The policies or rules governing specific fisheries
announced by the United States, or by other governments
in conformity with policies announced by the United
States with respect to coastal fisheries;
(4) The Japanese national and local regulations for
the conservation of fisheries.
SWNCC 99/3
- 16 -

=== Page 12 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
e. Such fishery products may be exported as can be
produced by vessels, facilities, gear, equipment and
supplies not suitable for or convertible to use in pro-
viding for domestic consumption, and which are needed
(1) to supply United Nations needs for animal proteins
and oils or (2) to secure foreign exchange for essential
imports.
f. In the establishment of local security regulations
consideration should be given to ensuring the maximum
production of seafood products consistent with security
requirements.
RECOMMENDATIONS
5. It is recommended that:
a. This report be referred to the Joint Chiefs of
Staff with a request for their comments from a military
point of view; and
b. Upon approval by the SWNCC of the "Conclusions" in
paragraph 4 above:
(1) This report be transmitted to the Joint Chiefa
of Staff and to the State, War and Navy Departments
for their guidance and, where appropriate, for imple-
mentation; and
(2) The "Conclusions" in paragraph 4 above be comnuni-
catod by the Fur Eastorn Advisory Coimission to the Govern-
ments of the United Kingdom, the Soviet Union, China,
France, the Comnonwealth of the Philippines, Australia,
Canada, New Zealand and the Netherlands together with
a statement that the U.S. Government has dispatched
the "Conclusions" to the Supreme Commander for the
Allied Powers.

APPENDIX "A"
\
FACTS BEARING ON THE PROBLEM
1. SWNCC 150/4 states in Part IV, 3, pages 56-7:
"Japan will be expected to prcvide goods and services
to meet the needs of the occupying forces to the extent that
this can be effected without cau ing starvation, widespread
disease and acute hysical distress.
"The Japanese authorities will be expected, and if
necessary directed, to maintain, develop and enforce pro-
grams that serve the following purposes:
(a)
To avoid acute economic distress.
(0)
To assure just and impartial distribution of
available supplies.
(c)
To meet the requirements for reparations deliveries
agreed upon by the Allied Governments.
(c)
To facilitate the restoration of Japanese economy
so that the reasonable peaceful recuirements of
the population can be satisfied.
"In this connection, the Japanese authorities on their
own responsibility shall be permitted to establish and ad-
minister controls over economic activities, including es-
sential national public services, finance, banking, and pro-
duction and distribution of essential commodities, subject
to the anproval and review of the Supreme Commander in order
to assure their conformity with the objectives of the oc-
cupation."

APPENDIX "B"
DISCUSSION
1. Befere the var Jrpan's fisheries prcduction was the
largest in the vorld. The annual catch of Japan Proper has amounted
to nearly 3 million tons. About 1 1/2 million people were engaged
either full or part time in the industry. Among important primary
fishery products are fis , shellfish, seaweed and marine ma mals.
The fisreries provide imoortant seurces of protein in the diet, as
well as sup lying much of the fertilizer, animal cils, fats and
scins. In 1937 the fiahing flret consisted of 364,260 vessels, com-
prising 397,961 without engines, 97 with steam engines and 66,202
with oil burning engines.
98% cf the vessels without motors and
73% of those with engines were under 5 tons.
Exports of fishery
products 1936-39 averaged 235,000 tons annually vith a value of
$30 million--over 3 1/2% of thr total value of Japanese cxports.
2. Proauction of fish durins the war bas decreased to an
catim tod catch of 2 million tons in 1943-44 and of 1.4 million
tons in 1944-45. This decline vould eprear to have had an impor-
tant of cct uron the Japanese dict. In 1935-37-39 fish contri-
butcd 85 (or 3.85) cut of the total of 270 calories.
In 1943-4
they contributed 2.8% of the 2050 calorica. Although fish con-
stitute e small proportion of the total caloric intake, they are
virtually the sole source of animal protein. In western countries
30% to 40% of the dict is of animal origin, while in Japen the
proportion is 10%--almost all frem thc fisheries. In the pre-var
period, consumpticn for food averaged 85 pounds per year per capita
of wholc fish; in 1943-44 oonsumpticn averaged 55 pounds.
S. It is clear that Japan should produce the volume of fishery
products required for her domestic consumption both of food and
fortilizer in order to relieve the United Nations of the burden
of feeding the Japanese populations and occupying forces during the
period of critical food shortages in the United Netions and liver-

4.
The extent to which Japen may be in a position to mect
the domestic requiroments imuediately following the cessation
of hostilities appears to be depondent primarily upon (a) the
condition of vesscls, supplies, equipment and geer and the avail-
ability of replacements, (b) the damage to the fisherics and to
the industrial capital cquiument through naval, military and air
action, and (c) availability of fuel.
5. It is dcsir-blr th-t cll Japanesf neval guxiliary and
fis ing vessels vhich are canablr of use in fishing oper:tions,
as well : s all grer, sumplies, and equipment in Japanese hands,
be employed es fully as possible in production to supply domestic
needs. Facilities ond materiels for repairs and replacements
may be drawn from Japanesc sources, but a portion may have to be
supplied from sources outside Jepan. Making such vessels, oar,
supplies and cquipment available carly will reduce materially the
food import requirements.
6. A portion of the production facilities may not readily
be converted to usr in production of the tyne and quality of
goods customary in the domestic markct. In general, luxury
products should not be produced for seneral domestic consumption
at a time when such items are not radily svailable in the world
market.
While it ic desirable to secure maximum production of
the luxury products when materials and facilities are availsble
and not useful for production of basic items, consideration
should be given in distribution of the products, not only to
customary domestic consumetion patterns, but also to the noeds
of the occupying forces, end requirements for the United Nations,
for forcign exchange and for shipping facilities.
7. Although it may be advantageous in long rang conomic
policy to reestablish and develop all Japencse fishing industrics,
including those engaged in export, it is preferable at present to
exploit the interior and coastal fisheries as the primary sources
for domestic consumption needs because, (a) they have, in the
past, provided the grert bulk of Japen's domestic requircmcnts,

and arc both a traditional and major source, (b) the fishing
and processing operotions are simple and require equipment and
facilitics, thc major portion of which might be produced locally,
(c) the enterprises, particularly in coastal waters, are for the
most part communal in character, and (a) international probloms
of rights to fisheries are largely avoided since the majority
of v sscls engaged in coastal fishing stay within 30 miles of
shore.
8. The trawling operations require mechanizrd equipment
and involve use of waters greater distances from Japan Proper
and noar the continent. The products of the Japancse concessions
in Soviet waters and the deep sea fisheries beforc the war were
primarily export itens.
9. Japan and the USSR concluded a five-year agreement com-
mencing January 1944, dealing with Japan's rights in the Soviet
fishcries. In r cent years, on the grounds of security, the
Soviet Government has curtailed tre enjoyment by Japan of those
rights W ich have been recognized since the Russo-Japancso War.
Legal and diplomatic considcrations vill possibly det rmine
the extent and conditions of xploitation of the concessions in
Soviet waters during the period of occunation.
10. Japan has taken stens to conserve her interior and
coastel fisheries resources. Maint nance of the Japancse cgu-
lations concerning closed ereas, open ond closed seasons, intonsity
of fishing, ete. appcar desirable in order to encourage the taking
of the maximum quantity of fish consistent with the pres rvation
of the fisheries.
11. It is necessary that Japan be prevented from conducting
fishing operations which will endanger the Pacific fisheries.
While Japan has r gulated the fisheries in home waters in the
inter sts of conserv tion, Japanese fishing operations in other
waters have had little regard for conservation. Progress is being
made toward establishing, in concert with other interested nations,

a system for protecting the fisheries, but it is not expected
that a program of regulation will be instituted in the immcdiate
future. As en interim measure, it is recommended that Japenese
fishing on the high scas along the Anerican coasts and off
American possessions be prohibited until an adequate consorvation
regime is ostablished. While thr recommendation applies only to
the continents of North and South Anerica and to the island pos-
sessions of the United States, it is rccognized that similar zones
for protcction of fisheries may be cstablished at the requost of
allied or noutral nations in other waters wherc the needs for
such protection of the fisheries is clearly evident.
12.
The whaline agreements in effect with respect to the
United States at the present time are: (1)
Convention for the
Regulation of Wheling sign d at Geneva, September 24, 1931, Treaty
Scrics s80; (2) Agreement for tre Rrgulation of Whaling, and
Final Act signed at London, June 8, 1937, Treaty Series 933;
(3) Protocol amending the Agrrenent for the Regulation of Whaling
signed at London, June 1937, and Final Act of the Conferencc
signed at London, June 24, 1938, Trcaty Series 944; Japan partic-
ipated in the conferences preparing the conventions but failed
to ratify.
13. The fur seal tre ty of 1911 (T.S. 546) with the United
States, Gr at Britain, Russia and Japan was terminated in
October 194l upon notice from Japan on the ground that Japen's
herds vere endangering her fisheries. Subsequently, Canada and
the Unitcd States concludcd an executive agreement (E.A.S. 415)
governing the Northeast Pacific area. Until the fects as to the
conditions of the Robben and Kurile Islands herds are establishcd,
any sealing which may be authorized by the povernment curing tho
occupation poriod should be undertaken in conformity with sound
conserv tion principles. All pelagic scaling should be prohibited.

My dear Mr. Griffin:
We have for acknowledgement your letter of December 14, 1945.
Since last writing you, we nave heard from Tokyo that seed oysters
will not be available for export from Japan before December 1946.
Between December 1946 and March 1947, it is anticipated that ten
thousand cases can be exported.
By this same mail, we are suggesting to the Honorable Charles
R. Savage, who has approached us on behalf of the Pacifio Coast
Oyster Growers Association, that the Association communicate with
the U. S. Commercial Company, 81l Vermont Avenue, Washington, D. C.
and express their interest in the importation of seed oysters from
Japan.
894.628/12-1445
For reasons of security it will not be possible immediately
for private firms in this country to deal directly with Japanese
business houses. Consequently, arrangements have been made whereby
the U. S. Commercial Company will handle imports from Japan for
sale to established importers.
Sincerely yours,

GENERAL
HEADQUARTERS
SUFREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
AG 080 (25 Cct 45) NR
APO 500
17 November 1945
Pacific Coast Oyster Growers Association,
202 First National Bank Building,
Olympia, Washington.
Gentlemen:
This letter is in further reply to your letter of 2 October
1945 on the subject of seed oysters from Japan.
We have now learned that there will be no exportable surplus
of seed oysters available from Japan in 1945. It is expected,
however, that some 10,000 cases, of 12,000 or more spat each, will
be available around December 1946 to March 1947. Availability of
these stocks will be dependent upon the export demand being indi-
cated within the next three or four months.
If you will advise this Headquarters of the probable demand,
we will so advise the seed-oyster producess.
FOR THE SUPHENE COMMANDER:
Harold Rair,
It. Col., A.G.D.,
Asst. Adjutant General.

PAULS' (sic) PEARLS
"Call Paul for Pearls"
31 N. State Stre@t
Chicago 2, Ill.
October 24th, 1945
State Department
Washington, D. C.
Dear Sirs:
I am an American citizen of Japanese descent, born in
Hawaii, and married to an American citizen of Spanish-French descent
and have three children. I am 39 year old. I have been in cultivated
pearl business for the past 15 years, and considered as an expert in
pearls.
I am writing this letter because I think you would be in-
terested in knowing about the cultivated pearls which are produced
only in Japan. It takes from 3 to 4 years to produce cultivated
pearls from oysters and only Japan was able to produce them because
Mr. K. Mikimoto was the inventor of this method of producing the
pearls from the living oysters, and also labor is cheaper than any
other country. If this pearls are to be cultivated in this country,
with high cost of labor, the pearls would cost just as high as the
Oriental pearls. The pearl industry in Japan was growing until the
war and I think it must have stop ed as America has been the main
importer of the same. This is one of the peaceful industry using
many people in Japan as first they must raise the oysters and then
must be scientifically treatea in order to bear the pearl in each
oysters. The treated oysters must be look after by many persons for
more than two to three years before they are able to obtain the pearls
from them. Then pearls has to be graded and matched for color and
shape to make up the necklaces; all pearls has to be drilled in the
large factories etc.
This industry will not only give many jobs for the people
in Japan but will encourage the peaceful industry. This industry
vill not deprive any livelihood from any Americans in this country
as we cannot possibly raise pearls here due to high labor cost.
I have worked under Mr. Mikimoto who is the inventor of
cultivated pearls and I have only handled cutivated pearls in this
country and considered as an expert in the pearls. I have liquidated
the entire cultivated pearls stock of Mikimoto under the Treasury
Department direction while I was in New York, and also made an appraisal
for the Alien Property Custodian in Chicago in regard to the cultivated

=== Page 61 === (confidence 0.99)
pearls, and I am sure that I have enough experience in the pearls.
If you are interested in having someone make an investigation
in regard to the cultivated pearl industry in Japan I would like to
have you appoint me for the task as I am sure that I can complete
the survey in this particular field in Japan in one month time if I
am permitted to go there, as I know many of the pearl growers and I
am in better position to discuss their problem as one pearl-man to
another, and I can speak their language fluently.
I have taught the Civil Affairs Training School for the
officers at the Chicago University in Japanese language, and after
the close of the school I am teaching the army enlisted men of A.S.T.P.
at the University of Chicago at the present time.
Hoping to have the pleasure of hearing from you in the
near future, I am
Sincerely yours,
(Signed)
Paul Kirimura
C
0
P

UNITED STATES POLITICAL ADVISER

1R
Tokyo, July 1, 1946.
CONFIDENTIAL
INTGESDIVISION

146
DE
STATE
SUBJECT: Extension of Japanese Fishing and Whaling Areas.
PN. M-JA-m-Emerson
36
SANCH
No outnd dishubulin
The Honorable
The Secretafy of State
Washington.
894.628/7-146
784
Sir:
C
I JHave the honor to refer to this Mission's despatch No. 464,
June 12, 1946, entitled, "Sixth Meeting of Allied Council for Japan,
1
June 12, 1946", and to enclose a copy of a memorandum of June 4,
1946, from the Acting Chief of Staff, General Headquarters, SCAP,
to the Allied Council for Japan, requesting comments of the Council
concerning a partial staff study pertaining to authorizing fishing
2/
and whaling by the Japanese, and a copy of the pertinent portions
of the verbatim minutes of the sixth meeting of the Council covering
discussion on this subject. There are also enclosed copies of a
3/
directive, SCAPIN 1033, June 22, 1946, to the Japanese Government on
the subject, "Area Authorized for Japanese Fishing and Whaling", of
4/
a. letter dated June 20, 1946, from Lieutenant General K. N. Derevyanko,
Member for the Union of Soviet Socialist Republics of the Council on
this subject, and of my reply, June 24, 1946, as Chairman of the
5/
Council.
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
General Derevyanko's questions in the Council meeting were prin-
DIVIBION OF FOREIAN
11946
cipally designed to elicit information on technical details, such as:
the volume of estimated excess of the fish-catch and destinations of
CS/V
such exportable excess; detailed comparisons of the Japanese fishing
fleet by types of vessels at the beginning of the war and at present;
information regarding new construction of fishing vessels, especially
steel vessels; numbers of fishing personnel and their registration;
and the possibility of distributing fishing vessels among the Allied
Powers. As a preliminary opinion, General Derevyanko stated during
the Council meeting of June 12: "in principle I have no objections to
permitting the Japanese to conduct fishing and whaling in the suggest-
ed area as a temporary arrangement".
In his letter of June 20, General Derevyanko indicated that his
Government had reversed him and was opposed to the extension of exist-
FILED
ing Japanese fishing areas. The reasons advanced were that (1) the
301946
present area allotted to the Japanese is sufficient to fulfil internal
Japanese demands, and (2 the extension of fishing areas for Japan is
related to the creation of a large ocean-going fishing fleet, the size
of which is subject to discussion by the Allies and the Far Eastern
Commission. In my reply of June 24, I mentioned that the estimated
28/7-146
catch mentioned in the Council meeting was largely speculative and
based

=== Page 107 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch 481,
July 1, 1946
2
-2-
based upon the average catch during pre-war years when the Japanese
fishing industry was in far better condition than today. I also
stated that I could perceive no connection between the size of the
post-war Japanese fishing fleet and the temporary emergency measure
under discussion.
The Soviet attitude toward this problem is difficult to reconcile
with the realities of the food situation in Japan today. The reversal
of General Derevyanko's preliminary approval by the Soviet Government
may be an indication of continued Soviet opposition to constructive
measures undertaken by SCAP to alleviate the serious food shortage.
In the light of the discussion concerning this problem which took place
in the sixth meeting of the Council, the reasons advanced in General
Derevyanko's letter appear specious and suggest a continued Soviet
desire to hamper the policies of the Occupation in Japan.
Respectfully yours,
George Atcheson, Jr.

SUAY OF USLO ABNG OF ...
At the Tolyo meetin, a proposal to extend the area in whicn the Japanese
are resently authorized to conuuct fishing and whaling was brought up for
discussion. Ine authorized area woula pe extenued south to 240 North Latitude,
C

The Norwegian Ambassador called this afternoon and inquired
regarding our attitude toward the resumption of whaling aotivities
by the Japanese. He said thet is government felt that the Japanese
should be prohibited from engaging in wh ling in the future because,
largely due to their disregard of international whaling agreements,
whales have be∞me so scarce that Japanese partioipation In the
whaling industry now would tend to make unprofitable the wheling
operation of the Norwegians and other United Nations nationals. I
told the Ambassador that in so far is I knew we had taken no firm
position on this question although we felt that Japan should be
allowed to develop a reasonable peuceful eo nomy which would naturally
include fishing. I explained that the polioy on this question would
normally oome within the jurisdiction of the Far Eastern Commission
and suggested, therefore, that he might care to talk informally with
the various national representatives of tho Comiission. I also
promised to consult varicus officers in the Department who might have
an interest in this matter and to inform him later if any definite

me and said that he would talk with sir George Sansom and other
representatives of the Com ission.

sUBJECT: Japanese Whaling Operations in Antarctic.
The attached memorandum gives the substance of a telephone
conversation with Mr. Graves this morning. Mr. Flory of IR/
Fisheries has prepared the memorandum on the basis of my report
of Mr. Graves' conversation with you on August 12. Mr. Flory's
memorandum statee as follows:
"With reference to your memorandum of August 14,
the following information (which is the same as that
supplied in reply to inquiries from the Norwegians) may
serve as a basis for your reply to Mr. Graves on this
subject:
FW
"SCAP was authorized to approve Japanese Antarctio
whaling subject to the following conditions:
894.628/8-15
"1. All operations to be in accordance with inter-
national agreements for the regulation of whaling to
which the U.S. is or may be party.
"2. Whale oil produced to be subject to IEFC
allocation.
"3. Approval for undertaking this expedition is
without prejudice to future determinations concerning
the propriety of Japanese whaling, or the disposition
of vessels, equipment and products."
46
IR has drafted a telegram to SCAP asking for verification of the
fact that he has authorized factory vessels to engage in whal-
ing operations and asking for detailed information regarding
the contemplated operations, methods of inspection, etc.
From Mr. Graves' conversation this morning I gather that
the British may take a very strong stand on this subject and
Fw
we will probably have to formulate a very definite policy
in the matter ourselves.

=== Page 151 === (confidence 0.99)
STANDARD FORM NO. 64
TO
FE: Mr. Vincent
DATE: 8-16-46
FROM : JA: Mr. Emmerson
suBject: Japanese Whaling Operations in Antarctic.
Mr. Graves of the British Embassy telephoned today to
inquire whether we could make any reply to his inquiries
with regard to Japanese whaling. I said that we could assure
him that the allocation of whale oil resulting from these
operations would be made by the IEFC. I seid that the Depart-
ment had wired SCAP for confirmation of the fact that he had
authorized Japanese to engage in whaling operations and that
we expected to have more detailed information within the near
future.
Mr. Graves stated that it was not a question of the alloca-
tion of whale oil but whether the Japanese should be permitted
to engage in whaling at all. he said that he had cabled his
Government following his conversation with you and that he
had received no instructions in the matter so he was speaking
now personally and not officially. However, he was convinced
that any authorization to the Japanese to engage in whaling
operations would be a violation of policy adopted by the FEC.
r. Graves referred to FEC O35 on "Fishing and Aquatic Industries
in Japan" anl he quoted part of paragraph b as follows:
"Japanese fishing should not be permitted near areas
under Allied jurisdiction without prior permission from the
country concerned. These prohibitions should continue until
international agreements are negotiated permitting Japanese
fishing in these areas."
Mr. Graves took the position that all international agreements
to which Japan is a party have been abrogated by the war and
therefore it was not a question of Japan carrying on operations
under any international agreement.
Mr. Graves stated that he had been informed by the
Norwegians that they understood "some sort of" authorization
had been given to SCAP by the War Department. He inquired
whether this was a fact. I told him we would make further
inquiry and communicate with him later on the subject.
JA:JKEmmerson:mls

rized for the 194s - 1947 season. Such operations will be conducted
only in that part of the Pacific and Indian Oceans of tha Antarctic
bounded by south latitude 55 degrees on the north and that part of the
Antarctic land mass on the south generally known as Wilkes Land, South
Victoria Land and the Ross Shelf and between east longitude 90 degrees
on the east of Greenwich and west longitude 170 degrees on the west of
Greonwich, herein fter referred to as the Antarctic Whaling Area, during
the period and under the conditions set forth by the International Whal-
ing Conventions or modifications thereof which may bo adopted prior to
the season.

: Whaling Industry.

1—1493
Mr. Graves brought up the question of Japanese whaling
operations in the Antarctic as approved by SCAP and stated
that since his conversation with Mr. Emmerson on August 12
he had discovered that the problem is much more complicated
than he had originally anticipated. He reported that appar-
cntly not only the United Kingdom but Norway, Australia and
New Zealand were much interested in the problem. Moreover,
as the whole question involved various branches of his Govern-
ment he probably woulc not be in a position to make any state-
ment on the subject until August 22.

=== Page 170 === (confidence 0.96)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Memorandum of Conversation
DATE: 8-21-46
SUBJECT:
Japanese Whaling Operations in Antarctic
PARticiPAnts: Major Plimsoll - Australian Legation
Mr. Vincent - FE
Mr. Borton - JA
COPIES TO:
JK - Mr. Martin
IR/Fisheries - Mr. Flory
8
6
GPO
1-1493
4
Major Plimsoll called at his request to report that he had
been asked to take up with the Department informally the question
of Japanese whaling operations in the Antarctic. He said that
2
his Government objected to these proposed operations for the
8
following reasons:
1
8
(1) Australia, because of her geographical position and
3-2146
possessions to the south, feels that the Antarctic is of direct
strategic importance to her;
(2) The extension of Japanese whaling to the Antarctic
appears to be counter to the provision in the United States policy
paper (FEC-O35) which states "Japanese fishing should not be per-
mitted near areas under Allied jurisdiction without prior permis-
sion from the country concerned.";
(3) As Japanese whaling operations in the Antarctic would
894.6
e near Australian territory, the Australian Government feels
that they should have been consulted or that the problem should
have been raised in the Allied Council in Tokyo;
C
(4) If the United States Government approves of the opera-
S
9
tions proposed by General MacArthur, the Australian Government
feels that such operations would be an infringement of Article 4
工
of the International Whaling Convention of November, 1945 to
9
which this country is a party. The Australian Government interprets
the proposed
146

=== Page 171 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
the proposed operation to mean either that the United States
intends to include the catch by the Japanese within the limits
laid down by the Convention which would necessitate a reduction
in shares of the signatories or the increase in the total catch
over and above the amount established by the Convention which
would be a violation of the Convention;
(5) The Australian Government is of the opinion that if
whaling facilities are available in Japan.they should be operated
by the Allies, preferably by the Commonwealth governments;
(6) The Australian Government is also concerned over allow-
ing Japanese to begin whaling operations as, once such operations
are begun, it would be very difficult to stop them and thus far
no decision has been made to begin such operation.
Major Plimsoll stated he wes raising the matter informally
and hoped that the case might be settled without their having
to make a formal protest on the matter. In answer to Major
Plimsoll's comments we said that we would look into the matter,
and inquire further of SCAP as to the relations of the expedi-
tion to the Convention of November 1945 and would report to him
as soon as a decision had been reached within the Government
on the matter.
43
JA:HBorton:mls

SUBJECT:
: Japanese Whaling Question in Antarctic.
In reference to the attached memorandum of conversation
with Major Plimsoll from Australia I have been going over
carefully the terms referred to in Atcheson's reply of July 29
(No. 340) and find that the situation is more confused than I
thought and I am suggesting that Martin of JK and Flory of
IR get together with me in the near future to see if we can
clarify the situation.
8
94.628
The developments as I see them are as follows:
(1) On April 1 Agriculture asked the Acting Political
Adviser whether the Japanese were planning to undertake any
wheling operations in the 1946-47 season;
8
(2) On April 17 CINCAFPAC reported that Japanese were
8
informally seeking attitude of SCAP in reference to whaling
-2
and that SCAP was favorably inclined to permit resumption of
Japanese whaling in Antarctic under proper restrictions and
y
146
regulations, "in accordance with all the terms and conditions
of the International Whaling Convention to which Japan was
directed to adhere on November 3 and requested the views of
War Department" (telegram No. 59997);
(3) On April 27 the War Department in telegram 86015
telegraphed CINCAFPAC that Antarctic whaling is controlled by
international agreements to which the U.S. is a party and that
number of wnales to be captured is also controlled. Telegram
further stated that resumption of whaling by Japanese will
7
require international Allied agreement, that U.S. position
was still under discussion and that "as soon as U.S. position
m
is firm consultations with other nations concerned will be
instituted. State now discussing problem and will advise
soonest.";
S
So far I have been unable to determine who instituted the
above cable in State and as far as I am aware no such consulta-
J
tions with other nations have been instituted by us.
(4) On May 14 a cable (No. 308) was subm1tted to POLAD,
Tokyo stating that subject to folloving conditions in addition
2
to provision of SWNCc, Department perceives no objection on
policy grounds
0
6

=== Page 175 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
policy grounds to Japanese pelagic whaling in Antarctic. The
conditions were whale oil produced to be subject to CFB alloca-
tion and approval for undertaking this expedition withoutl
prejudice  future decision regarding disposition of vessels,
equipment and products. SCAP was asked to advise promptly if
and when it approves to enable Department to review suggested
provisions for compliance with treaty obligations for inspection
and enforcement;
(5) SCAP cabled May 2Oth (No. CX 61165) that SCAP wil1
obtain reasonable assurance of effective production before
approval and State Department will be advised of action taken
by SCAP;
(6) On June 7 (No. Z O1742) SCAP requested ful1 text of
International haling Convention with regulations for 1945-46
season and 46-47 season;
(7) On July 25 (No. 413) POLAD was instructed that the
Horwegian Government has expressed the view that Japan should
be prohibited from future whaling and that the Department plans
no reply pending SCAP decision authorized in Department telegram
308 of May 14;
(8) On August 2 (No. 426) to Political Adviser stated
that Russians had raised FEC objection to extension of Japanese
fishing in whaling areas and that FEC Sub-comnittee desired
additional information on matter;
(9) On August 6 (No. C63799) SCAP reported his plans
reference authorization of two factory ships and twelve killer
vessels to operate in Antarctic in 1946-47 season and stated
provisions for compliance with treaty obligtions for inspec-
tion and enforcement woula be forwarded when finally formulated.

THE FOREIGN SERVICE
OF THE
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
No. 1420
AMERICAN LEGATION
CONFIDENTIAL
Canberra, Australia, August 22, 1946.
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
REPORTING SERVICES
DIVISION OF FOREIGN
CEP 19 1346
Subjeot: Request from Political Adviser to
General MaoArthur that Legation
Release Statement to Australian
Press Regarding Whaling by Japanese
in Antarotic.
894.628/
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to report that on August 20, 1946
00
the Legation received a telegram from Mr. George Atoheson,
1
Politioal Adviser to General MacArthur, requesting that
2
246
it give to the Australian press a statement made by a
spokesman for the Supreme Commander in Tokyo explaining
the reasons for permission having been granted to the
Japanese to conduct whaling operations in the Antarotio.
/1
A copy of this telegram is enclosed.
IGN
The granting of permission by the Supreme Commander
COPIES
in Tokyo to the Japanese to fish for whales in the
102
Antarotio has been the subject of numerous press reports
元/1
and comment. It has been stated that Mr. W. MoMahon Ball,
the British Commonwealth representative on the Allied
Council for Japan, has protested that General MacArthur
1 BC
authorized Japanese whaling operations in the Antarotic
/ FEC(ERD)
without having sought the opinion of the Allied Council
for Japan. The Prime Minister has stated that the Aus-
/DRF
tralien Government and the Governments of the United
Kingdom and New Zealand were discussing the question of
Japan reentering the whaling industry in the Antarotio.
General MacArthur's action has served to renew press
discussion with respect to the advisability of Australia
entering the whaling industry and some of the leading
metropolitan dal lies have been rather critioal of the
Government for its failure actively to push the matter.
In view of the somewhat controversial nature of the
statement made by General MacArthur's spokesman, as given
in Mr. Atcheson's telegram, the Legation considered it
advisable informally to seek the opinion of the Department
3
of
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 180 === (confidence 0.99)
AIR MAIL
-2-
of External Affairs with respect to its reaction to
the release to the Australian press by the Legation
of the statement. It learned that such action would
not be regarded with particular favor by the Depart-
ment of External Affairs and consequently it has re-
frained from taking the action requested by Mr. Atcheson.
In this connection it might be well to review
briefly for the Department's information an incident
which occurred in March in connection with the repatri-
ation from Australia of a number of Formosan and Korean
internees on board a demilitarized Japanese destroyer,
the Yoizuki. At that time the metropolitan press was
bitterly oritical of the Government for having permitted
the alleged over-loading of the Yoizuki with men, women
and ohildren and accused the Government of having en-
deavored to place the onus for this occurrence on General
MacArthur whose headquarters had supplied the vessel for
the repatriation. Regardless of the rights and wrongs of
the matter, it was utilized here for internal political
purposes since it afforded the anti-Government metro-
politan dailies an exoellent opportunity roundly to
oritioize the Government. At that time General MacArthur,
presumably to clarify his position in the matter, released
to the Australian press through United States Army author-
ities in Australia a statement giving his position in the
/2
matter. A copy of this statement is enclosed. The United
States Army authorities in Australia, without reference
to the Legation, made the statement public. It was most
embarrassing to the Government and the Minister for External
Affairs, Dr. Evatt, personally complained to the Legation,
pointing out the impropriety on the part of the United
States Army authorities issuing in Australia statements
of a controversial nature which were embarrassing to the
Government.
Another incident of a similer character, which has
occurred here recently, is that of the statement made to
the press by the Dutch Minister with reference to the
Piet Hein (see Legation's despatch no. 1365 of June 19,
1946 and no. 1369 of June 26, 1946 entit1ed Australian-
Dutch Relations) which resulted in the Dutch Minister
receiving a public rebuke from the Prime Minister in
Parliament for having eiroumvented regular diplomatic
channels and appealed directly to the Australian public.
Bearing in mind the above it is my considered opinion
that the Legation would be most ill-advised to make any
release to the Australian press along the lines of the
statement made by General MacArthur's spokesman in Tokyo.
Any useful advantage which might be gained through publicity
under the Legation's auspices of the reasons motivating
General MacArthur's action would be more than counter-
balanced by the damage which would be done to the now
most
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 181 === (confidence 0.99)
AIR MAIL
-3-
most cordial relations existing between this Mission
and the Commonwealth Government. It might be pointed
out, moreover, that statements made in Tokyo by spokesmen
of the Supreme Commander which have a bearing on Aus-
tralia are fully reported in the Australian press.
I would appreciate receiving instructions from the
Department indicating what action, if any, I should take
in the event that I should receive a similar request from
the Political Adviser to the Supreme Commander in the
future.
Respectfully yours,
H. Earb Russell
H. Earle Russell
Charge d'Affaires ad interim

on Yoizuki incident, released
by U.S. Army authorities to

Message received through Harman Naval Wireless Station,

DMPORTANT
From:

American Legation, Canberra.

ZE13661
Roquest you give to Australian papers following
statement made by spokesman for Supreme Commander.
(This is Atcheson unnumbered, August 19.)
"The permission given the Japanese to fish for
whale in the Antarotic region for the coming winter
is a purely humanitarian measure. It is to help
provide the food for Japan which that country cannot
provide for itself. The United States has found it
necessary to assume the burden of making up food
deficits for the Japanese to its own detriment and
consequently authorized General MacArthur, if he
deemed it advisable for the safoguarding of the
ocoupation, to permit these temporary whaling oper-
ations. General MacArthur at once sanctioned the
catch for the next season espeoially stipulating that
the decision had no bearing on future international
whaling regulations as directed. The operation in-
fringes in no way upon the rights or interests of any
nation."

Enclosure no. 2 to despatch no. 1420, dated August 22,
1946, from American Legation, Canberra, Australia,
on subject of "Request from Political Adviser to

to Australian Press Regarding Whaling by Japanese

Statement released to Australian
press by U. S. Army in Australia
in behalf of General MacArthur
GHQ in Japan has no responsibility for the
repatriation of enemy nationals, prisoners of war
or others, from the Australian Žone other than to
furnish the vessels with a designated passenger
capacity. All details of the loading of the vessels -
the actual selection of the passengers, their assign-
ment and all other matters inoident to their passage,
is the complete responsibility of the Australian Foroes
which function entirely under their own command. The
radios which passed between GHQ and the Australian
Forces with reference to the Yoizuki's trip were as
follows.
On l0 February from Australian Landforces, eite
UHZE M 7904 requesting vessel to lift 948 passengers:
"Will concentrate Formosans and Koreans at Sydney when
shipping made available total to be moved 948."

for discussion of the consider tions involved in working
2346
out an answer to objections that have been raised by Norway,

ese whaling expedition t the Antarctic authorized by SCAP

constitute the basis for formulation of the United States'
position in the event thet the whole problem is taken up
with Far Eestern Commission.

must be explored:
1.

conventions? This raises the cuestion as to whether
Americen inspectors should be placed on fectory
ships.
2.
Will the prospe ctive Je anese catch be included
in the existing overall quota (16,00o blue whale
units), or will it be in excess of the ouota?
3.

STATE
AIDE-MEMOIRE
1.
The New Zealand Government has been lead to believe, through press reports
and other sources, that the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers in Japan has
issued a directive authorizing the sending to the Antarctic for the 1946-47
whaling season of a Japanese whaling fleet of 12 whalers, 2 factory ships and 7
carriers.
2.
The area in which it is believed that this fleet hes been instructed to
operate is naturally of great importance to New Zealand because of its close
proximity to New Zealand shores and also to those of the Ross Dependency, in
respect of which New Zealand is the controlling authority.
3.
The past record of the Japenese in respect of whaling in the Antarctic is
one of failure to accede to the International Conventions regarding the taking of
894.628/8-2346
whales, and of predatory expeditions which threatened the continued existence of
the whaling industry and of the whale itself.
4.
The operation of Japanese vessels in such an erea will also raise security
questions for New Zealend, the details of which it is not necessery to mention at
the moment, but which the New Zealand Government view with some apprehension.
5.
The New Zealand Government therefore dislikes the prospect of the resumption
even temporarily, of Japanese whaling activities in Anrarctic waters, and it
CS/HH
considers it most important that no action should be taken which might prejudice
any ultimate policy decision concerning Japanese re-admission to, or exclusion from,
the Antarctic whaling industry. If, as now seems to be the case, Japan does in
fact appeer to have whaling facilities available for operetion, it is possible that
the difficulties might be met by some form of Allied operetion.
6.
Finally, the New Zealand Government wishes to place on record its surprise
that, on a matter of such great importance to New Zealand, it was not consulted,
0
either through the Allied Council or directly, before the issue of the Directiv
FILED
by the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers.
0
1946
New Zealand Legation,
Washington, D.C.

AIDEMÉMOIRE
The aide-mémoire of August 23, 1946, presented
by the Legation of New Zealand, which sets forth the
concern of the Government of New Zealand at the pro-
jected whaling expedition by Japanese vessels in the
8
Antarctic region has been studied carefully. The
394.628/8-2346
interest and concern of the Government of New Zealand,
partioularly with regard to the security factors
involved, in the extension of Japanese whaling into
the Antarctic is understood and appreciated. However,
it 1s the opinion of the Government of the United
O
0
/HH
States that when the reasons back of the approval of
this expedition as well as the manner in which it 1s
to be carried out are made clear, much of this concern
will be dissipated.
8946
It is desired to point out that the authorization
of the expedition by the Supreme Commander for the
Allied
人
3
F
6

=== Page 191 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
Allied Powers was an administrative action taken under
Directive No. 20A of November 13, 1945, which has been
filed with the Far Fastern Commission in accordance
with Paragraph III,4, of its terms of reference and
1s also contained in FEC-O35. This directive, 1n
paragraph d(1), clearly foresaw the possibility of the
Japanese being permitted to engage in vhaling under
the authorization of the Supreme Commander, and as
no member of the Far Eastern Commission had requested
the review of this directive, as provided for in
paragraph II A, 2, of the terms of reference, the
action of the Supreme Commander in approving the
expedition was clearly within his authority. This
approval was given in the hope that it would help to
alleviate the critical world shortage of fate and oils
and that it would elso contribute to the food supplies
of Japan. This latter factor 1s of partioular
importance to this Government because of the relation-
ship of the food shortage in Japan to the problems
of the

=== Page 192 === (confidence 0.97)
-3-
of the ocoupation. With respeot to the whale oil which
will be produced by the expedition this Government
desires to state that all of 1t, not merely the
exportable surolus, will be subject to allocation by
the International Emergency Food Counoil.
The Government of the United States agrees with
the Government of New Zealand that the past record of
the Japanese in respect of whaling in the Antarctio 1s
bad and in order to ensure against repetition of action
in violation of international whaling agreements, striot
orders have been issued that all provisions of such
agreements shall be observed in their entirety. The
Supreme Commander has stated that personnel from his
headquarters will accompany the expeuition to see that
these orders are carried out.
The Supreme Commander has also stated he would
heartily elcome the assignment of additional Allied
inspectors to the forthooming whaling expedition should
any of the nations concerned wish to offer the services
of such

=== Page 193 === (confidence 0.97)
一4一
of suoh persons. According to information furnished by
the Supreme Commander the maximum number of Allied
personnel poesible to acoommodate on each factory ship
is three and it is therefore possible for two Allied
inspectors to be essigned to each ship in addition to
the inspector from the Supreme Commander's headquarters.
In this connection the Supreme Commander states
it is important to point out to the interested govern-
ments that accommodations and meals will neoessarily
be sub-standard. These ships are converted Japanese
tankers with the usual minimum accommodations built for
Japanese personnel. Allied inspectors must be prepared
for cons1derable personal hardship and therefore 1t is
suggested that individuals selected should, in addition
to their professional cualifications, be of a type
capable of meeting these conditione in good spirit.
The Supreme Commander hes requested that he be
1nformed as soon as possible when any Allied inspectors
may be expected to report to the Fisheries Division,
Natural

Natural Resources Section of his headquarters. It

end rations of such inspectors as they may assign for

advisable to have equality of pay for all foreign
inspectors it is requested that, if it is desired to
appoint such inspectors, the Supreme Commander be
notified es soon as possible of their salarles.
The question of security which 1s emphasized by
the Government of New Zealand has received considerable
attention. This Government has been informed by the
Supreme Commander thet all Japanese personnel on the
expedition ill be carefully screened by intelligence
officers from his headquarters before being allowed to
embark. In addition, Japenese whaling vessels have
been prohibited from approaching closer than 12 miles
to any land beyond the home 1slands. The northern
boundary of the area in which whaling is authorized
is several

is several hundred miles distant from the metropolitan
areas of New Zealand, Australia or Tasmania. Fifteen

Supreme Commander will be notified of the route which
1t 1s proposed to take to the whaling grounds. This
information can be fur ished the Government of New
ealand should it so desire, and if it should wieh to
provide further seourity safeguards while the vessels
are passing New Zealend territory at a distance greater

It 1s emphasized that the present proposed expedition
is of an emergeney character, that it has been authorized
for one season only and thst in no way does it prejudice

re-admission to, or exclusion from, the Antarotio

by the Supreme Commander and will be acoompanied by
personnel from his headquarters. The ships on the
expedition will not fly the Japenese flag but will fly
a special pennant identifiable as follows: SCaJAP flag
4 feet 6 inches wide by 5 feet ? inches long, divided
diegonally into two right angle triangles from upper
left to lower right corners with lower triangle green
and upper triange red. The SCAJAP flag resembles the
international flag "O" with the exception that the lower
triangle on the SCAJAP flag is green instead of yellow
as on the international flag "O".
Because of the strong interest expressed in this
matter by the New Zealand and other governments the
Supreme

approved without prior consultation with the various
governments concerned, particularly concerning the
security aspects involved.

hope of the Government of the United States that the
objectione of the Government of New Zealand to the
proposed expedition may be withdrawn.

AIDE MEMOIRE
On the 12th August tnis Fmbassy enquired of the State
Department whether information was availeble on the reported in-
tention of the Supreme Commander for tre Allied Powers to author-
ise a Japenese whaling expedition to the Antarctic.
2.
His Mejesty's Government have now been apprised of the
text of S.C.A.P. Directive No. AG 800.217 (6 August 46), in
which permission is given for the Japanese to conduct whaling
894.628/9-346
operations in the Anterctic, and they cannot refrain from express-
ing their profound concern at the manner in which these proposals
have so far taken shape. They desire to draw the attention of the
State Department to the fourth resolution of the Whaling Conference 
held in London in November 1945, under which all parties of the
Agreement resolved to take steps to prohibit the sale, loan or
delivery of vessels equipped for whaling to countries not signa-
tories to the wheling Agreement, and t record their opinion that
the suggested plan is clearly contrary to the spirit, if not
perhaps the letter, of that resolution.
C/A
3.
Despite assumptions which may be made in & contrary sense
His Majesty's Government are most apprehensive that a Japanese
whaling expedition will, in fact, constitute a precedent and in
the light of the extremely bad whaling record of the Japanese it
is considered that the charscter of the expedition should be rad-
icelly changed. Furtiermore His Majesty's Government have reason
to suppose that the Japanese desire is largely to obtein whale
meat, in pursuance of which it is most probable that the whalers
would, as they have repeatedly done in the past, kill young whales.
4.
In viev of the critical oils and fats situation His
Majesty's Government would however be prepared to acquiesce in an
expedition/

=== Page 325 === (confidence 0.98)
expedition provided the following minimum safeguards were imposed:-
(a) that the expedition should not be a Japanese expedition
but should be run by S.C.A.P. under the control of allied
personnel.
(b) that all the oil should be subject to I.E.F.C. allocation
and not merely the residue after Japanese needs have been
inet.
(c) that the exnedition should not constitute a precedent,
nor tht it snould prejulice the possibility of Japanese
ships comprising the expedition being taken as reparations.
(0)
that the International Whaling Agreement should be observed
and, in particular, th:t any meat used should be only from
fully grown whales.
(e)
that each factory ship should be allocated a quota of whales
which, in no circumstances, should be exceeded.
(f)
that whaling should be conducted in accordance with modern
oie
whaling practice to ensure the extraction of all l and
the abandonment of the wasteful methods formerly used by the
Japanese.
(g)
that qualified allied inspectors and other personnel referred
to in paragraph five should be present.
5.
As regards safeguard (a) His Majesty's Government and, they
have reason to think the Norwegian Government, would be willing to
provide technicians, including masters and officers for the fectory
ships. Arrangements would be made to send such officers and tech-
nicians by air to meet the expedition. There would be no objection
to the remainder of the crews being Japanese.
His Majesty's Covernment are aditionally not satisfied that
there would be adequate control over the catcher boats and they regard
it as essential that there should be a Norwegian gunner, mate and
engineer on each catcher.
6.1

=== Page 326 === (confidence 0.99)
6.
His Majesty's Government suggest that the returns
the
from the sale of the catch be divided between Supreme
Comnander for the Allied Powers and the operating parties.
7.
His Majesty's Government would be grateful if, in
the light of the foregoing representations, the State Department
would arrange for appropriate instructions to be sent to the
Supreme Commander in or er that the expedition may be organised
withir the safeguards herein sug ested.
8.
Information has also been received to the effect that
in & directive to the Jaranese Government dated 23rd August the
Suprene Commander har given permission for the conversion of a
partly built tanker into & whale proceosing ship. In the
opinion of His ajesty's Government this proposal completely
undermines the ground on which the Supreme Commander based his
justification for tne 1946/47 whaling expedition, namely the
iir iediate urgency for fats and meat and his denial that it
prejudiced the future. Whatever may be said about this year's
ex edition, there can be no justification for the rehabilitation
of a Je penese whalinr industrywithout the fullest consultation
w!th all interested Governments.
His Majesty's Government accoraingly wish to express
their dissatisfection at this ner development and request the
United States Government to secure cencellation of this directive
and to issuo ina muctions to the Supreme Commander thet no further
decisions affecting whaling be taken without prior consultation.
BRITISH EMBASSY
WASHINGTON, D.C.
3 September, 1946

AIDENFPOIRE
The Department of State has noted carefully the
points raised in the British tmbassy's aide-mémoire of
September 3, 1946, regerding the subject of Japanese
whaling, in the Anterctio and desires to make the
894.628/9-546
following observetions.
The authorization of the projected Japenese whaling
expedition to the ntarctic vas an administretive action
taken by the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers under
irective No. 20A of November 13, 1945, which has been
CS/HH
filed with the Far astern Commiesion in eccordance with
paragraph I1I,4, of its terms of reference and is con-
tained in FiC-O35. This expedition was approved by the
supreme Commander definitely as an emergency measure and
for one season only in the hope that it would help to
C.
alleviate the critical world shortege of fats and oils
and that
F
6

=== Page 328 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
and that it would also contribute to the food supply of
Japan. This latter faotor 1s of particular importanoe
because of the relationship of the food shortage in
Japan to the problems of the oocupation. In this conneo-
tion the United States Government wishes to assure the
British Government thet all the whale oil produced by
this expedition, not merely the exportable surplus, will
be subject to allocation by the International Emergenoy
Food Council.
It is the intention of the United States Government,
and it has been so ordered in directives issued to the
Japanese Govern ent by the Supreme Commander, that the
proposed whaling expedition be carried out in full con-
formity with all the international conventions and regula-
tione pertaining to whaling. The Department of State
has been 1nformed by the Supreme Commander that personnel
from his commend will accompany the expedition to ensure
compliance with all international regulations. In view
of the above the United States Government does not consider
that it

=== Page 329 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
that 1t has violated the spirit of the fourth resolution
of the Final Act of the International Whaling Conference
of 1945. In the recollection of members of the United
States delegation to that Conference this resolution
was a recommendation to the contracting governments
with the purpose and intent of urging restraint in
facilitating the outfitting and equipping of whaling
expeditions which would not operate in conformity with
the rules end standards set forth in the various whaling
agreements.
The Supreme Commander has stated he would heartily
welcome the assignment of additional Allied inspectors
to the forthcoming whaling expedition should any of the
nations concerned wish to offer the services of such
persons. According to information furniehed by the
Supreme Commander the maximum number of Allied personnel
possible to accommodate on each factory ship 1s three
and it is therefore possible for two Allied inspectors
to be assigned to each ship in addition to the inspector
from the Supreme Commander's headquarters.
In th1s

=== Page 330 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
In this connection the Supreme Commander states it
1s 1mportant to point out to the interested governments
that accommodations and meals will necessarily be sub-
standard. These ships are converted Japanese tankers
with the usual minimum accommodations built for Japanese
personnel. Allied inepectors must be prepared for con-
siderable personal hardship and therefore it 1s suggested
thet individuels selected should, in addition to their
professional qualifications, be of e type capable of
meeting these conditions in good spirit.
The Supreme Commander has requested that he be
informed as soon as possible when any Allied inspectors
may be ex ected to report to the Fisheries Division,
Natural Resources Section, of his headquarters. It is
understood that foreign governments concerned will pay
the ealaries and provide the necessary equipment and
rations of such inspectors as they may assign for duty
with the expedition. Inasmuch as it 1s believed advisable
to have equality of pay for all foreign inspeotors it
is requested that, if it is desired to appoint such
inspeotors,

=== Page 331 === (confidence 0.98)
-5
inspectors, the Supreme Commander be notified as soon
as possible of their salaries.
The United States Government wishes to emphasize
that the proposed whaling expedition is of an emergeney
character only and in no way prejudices the future. This
Government is in complete accord with the view that
any long-range consideration of the rights of Japan to
conduct wheling onerations is a matter for Allied dis-
cussion and decision. With regard to the Supreme Com-
mander's directive of August 23 authorizing the conver-
sion of a tanker which will not be completed until the
spring of 1947, the Department has been informed that
thile such uthorization has been granted, no authoriza-
tion has been granted for the vessel to whale in the
Antaretic and no commitment has been made as to its
future use for this purpose or its exemption from repara-
tions removels. The ultimate disposition of this vessel,
indeed the ultimate disposition of all Japanese whaling
facilitles nd equipment 1s agreed to be subjeot to
Allied consultation and decision. The present
authorization

=== Page 332 === (confidence 0.99)
-6
authorization being only temporary, the Japanese have
been advised that any equipment they may possess, whether
then available or in process of construotion, is subjeet
to such disposition as may be determined by the Allied
Powers. Nor does this authorization in any way commit
the United States Government to support future whaling
operations by the Jananese.
With regard to the suggestion that masters and
officers of the expedition should be seleoted from
Allied personnel it is desired to emphasize, as stated
above, that the expedition will be accompanied by
personnel from the headquerters of the Supreme Commander
and 1f desired by other Allied inspectors whose duties
will be to see that all international whaling regulations
are faithfully observed. The Supreme Commsnder has
expressed the opinion that, in addition to the unusuelly
small size of the quarters pointed out above, technical
engineering peculiarities and the fact that their equip-
ment is not of the type ordinarily used by Europeans,
it woula

=== Page 333 === (confidence 0.98)
-7-
it would be inefficient to operate the ships to be used
on the expedition by other than Japanese crews. Besause
of the shortness of time before the expedition must set
out it is not considered practical to attempt to convert
the ships for European use.
It has been estimated by the Supreme Commander that
the total catch of the forthcoming expedition will not
exceed an equivalent of 1500 blue whale units. In view
of the anparent detailed planning which has been carried
out by the Supreme Commander and as every precaution
is being taken to ensure complience with all international
wheling agreements and regulations, 1t 1s not believed
advisable to impose a further restriotion, not called
for by ary international agreement, namely a quota upon
each factory ship as suggested in the aide-mémoire from
the British Embassy.
Because of the great interest expressed by the
British as well as other governments, particularly with
respect to the security interests involved, the Supreme
Commander

=== Page 334 === (confidence 0.98)
-8
Commander has informed the Department of State that no
authorization for any possible future Japanese whaling
expeditions in the Antarctic or elsewhere will be
given without prior consultation with the interested
governmente, particularly with regard to the seourity
faotors that may be involved.
The United States Government wishes to point out
that the Supreme Commander has stated the proposed
expedition 1s an Allied expedition under complete Allied
control. It 1s administered by the Supreme Commander
and will be accompanied by personnel from his head-
quarters as well as by other Allied personnel should
it be so desired. The ships on the expedition will not
fly the Japanese flag but will fly a special pennant
identifiable as follows: SCAJAP flag 4 feet 6 inches
wide by 5 feet 7 inches long, divided diagonally into
two right angle triangles from upper left to lower right
corners with lower trfangle green and upper triangle red.
The SCAJAP

=== Page 335 === (confidence 0.89)
-9-
The SCAJAP flag resembles the international flag "O"
with the exception that the lower triangle on the
SCAJAP flag 1s green instead of yellow as on the
international flag "O".
In view of the considerations expressed above
the United States Government hopes that the British
Government will aee fit to wi thdraw 1 ts objeotions
to the contemplated expedition.

Mr. Powles called to discuss the question of a Japanese
whaling expedition to the Antarctic authorized by SCAP. He
stated that hie government had been advised of the British aid
memoine of 3 September 1946 and was instructed to say that his
government agreed in general with the position presented by the
United Kingdom.
Mr. Powles added, however, that his government questioned
the advisability of restricting the gunner, mate and engineer on
each catcher to Norwegians, that they questioned the necessity of
C
dividing the returns from the sale of the catch as provided in
S
paragraph 6, and that they would be in a position to provide a
V
l1mited number of technical personnel for the expedition.
As to the query as to whether the question should be settled
outside of the Far Eastern Commission, Mr. Powles stated that his
present instructions were to defer raising the question in FEC.
Mr. Powles was informed that the whole subject of policyon
Japanese whaling was now under careful consideration in the D
FILED
894.628
partment and that he would be informed of the position of this
government as soon as such a decision was reached.

SUBJECT:
Japanese Whaling in the Antarctic
Mr.. Lykke, Counselor of the Norwegian Embassy

Mr. Lykke called to present an Aide-Memoire from his
Government on the subject of permitting the Japanese to engage
in whaling operations in the Antarctic. The note takes
exception in several points to the British position as set
forth in the Aide-Memoire presented by Mr. Graves of the
British Embassy to the Department on September 3.
S
Mr. Lykke explained that some discussions had been held
in London between the Norwegien Embassy and the British Foreign
Office but that apparently the attitude of the Norwegian
Government had not been correctly interpreted. He regretted
that this prevented his Government from presenting a "united
front" with the British and indicated that he would have further
discussions with Mr. Graves in an attempt to reach some agreement.
The Norwegians are unwilling to participate in any whaling
expedition where crew members would be Japanese or where ships
would sail under a Japanese flag. They furthermore oppose the
British contention that a limitation should be placed upon the
number of whales to be processed by participating factorships.

JAPANESE WHALING IN THE ANTARCTIC
Sopt 13, 1946
894.628/9-1346
To
11:30 a.m.
Certain American Diplomatic Officers.
The following summary of the background and development of the contro-
versy relating to the authorization by SCaP of a Japanese whaling expedi-
tion to the Antarctic during the season 1946-47 is transmitted for the in-
formation of certain American diplomatic officers only.
On April 17, 1946 SCAP informed the Department that Japanese whaling
interests had been informally seeking the attitude of SCap toward resumption
of whaling in the Antarctic. In view of the world need for fats and oils
and of the shortage of food in Japan, SCAp was favorably inclined to permit
a temporary resumption of Japanese whaling, under proper restrictions and
regulations, and requested the Department's views.
On May 14 SCAP was informed that
CS/A
"Subject to the following conditions in addition to provisions of
SWNCC Policy with Respect to Fisheries and Aquatic Industries in Japan;
Dept perceives no objection on policy grounds to resumption Jap pelagic
vhaling in Antarctic:
"I. Whale oil produced to be subject to CFB allocation...
"2. Approval for undertaking this expedition is without prejudice
to future decisions regarding disposition of vessels, equipment
and products." (Copy SWNCc Policy follows air mail.)
The Department also suggested that assurance of reasonably effective pro
duction should be obtained prior to SCAP ∞nsent to Japanese operations.
On August 6, SCAP issued a directive to the Japanese Govt., authorizing
4
two factory ships of 10,000 tons each, 12 killer vessels and 7 carriers to
operate in the Antarctic during the 1946-47 season. The directive indu des
the safeguards specified in the Dept's telegram of May 14. On August 8,
an Associated Press report of the authorigation appeared in the New York
Herald Tribune, and apparently other press reports and radio broadcasts
carried the story.
Shortly
4.174

=== Page 351 === (confidence 0.97)
TO BE TRANSMITTED

AIRGRAM SENT

RESTRICTED
Department of State
CONFIDENTIAL
Washington,
NO.
TIME
- 2 - 80pt 13, 1946
11:30 a.m.
Shortly after the appearance of this publicity the Charge d'Affaires
of the Norwegian Embassy inquired by telephone whether the Department
would confirm or deny reports. On August 19 the Cherge d'Affaires pre
sented orally Norway's objections to the expedition. The Australian
Embassy presented oral objections on August 2l. Aides memoire were sub-
mitted to the Department by the New Zealand Legation on August 23 and by
the British Embassy on September 3.
NC
The major objections specified in these protests are summarized below:
1. The failure of the Japanese in the past to observe whaling regula
tions or good whaling practices.
2. Concern lest this expedition constitute a precedent for further
Japanese Antarctic whaling operations.
3. Proximity of operations to New Zealand and Australian territorial
claims.
4. Failure to consult with other governments interested in whaling.
5. Factory and killer vessels have potential war use.
6. Japanese whaling equipment should be made available for use by
Allied nations.
Resolution 4 of the Whaling Conference of November 1945.
The British aide-memoire states that His Majesty's Government would
however "acquiesce in" the expedition provided it be operated by SCAP,
under the control of Allied personnel. It further states that a reported
ScAP approval of conversion of a tanker into a whale processing ship, such
conversion to be completed in 1947, "undermines the ground on which the
Supreme Commander based his justification for the 1946-47 whaling
expedition."
In viev: of the foregoing protests, the Department is exploring the
12.
legal and other aspects of this problem preparatory to formulation of
[2]
the Department's replies to the objections

Detailed information requested by Dept (REDEPTEL 476,
September 10 follows in separate telegrem).
It is believed thet the contentions of the foreign
governments, which seem to arise chiefly from Australian and
other commercial ambition, can readily be met.
This question has now become in Japan one involving
American prestige and heving important political implications.
The expedition is generally understood by Japanese to be an
American approved project arising primarily from the exigencies
of world wide food shortage and partly from the food shortage
in Japan. Any reversal in the matter would be interpreted
by the Japanese as clear indication that our predominant role
end authority in the occupation no longer exists and that
executive action is not rirm but is, rather, subject to dero-
gation upon unjustified foreign representation.
American prestige and political effectiveness have never
been higher in Japen than they are today. The present Japanese
Govt, for all its faults, has initiative, and is cooperat ng
by taking needed action in important matters upon suggeston
and without being ordered to do so. Any reversal on a mater
involving divergence of international views which has receiyed
