=== Page 366 === (confidence 0.97)
EMBASSY
AUSTRALIAN
WASHINGTON 8, D. C.
16th September, 1946.
Aide Memoire on Japanese Whaling
1.
On 21 August the Australian member of the Far Eastern
Commission made informal representations to the Director of
the Office of Far Eastern Affairs along the following lines
in regard to the extension of Japanese whaling into the
Antarctic:
(a) Australia is greatly concerned at the proposal for
the extension of Japanese whaling into waters of
direct and permanent security interest to Australia,
89
without prior consultation with interested governments
94.62
in accordance with the policy laid down by United
States and circulated to the Far Eastern Commission
2
8
as FEC-035.
6
(b) The matter appears to be one on which the Allied Council
1
1646
for Japan should have been consulted and asked to
advise.
(c) Approval by the United States Government of the Supreme
Commander's action seems contrary to the spirit of
the fourth resolution of the Final Act of the Inter-
national Whaling Conference of November, 1945.
(d) Australia considers that any whaling facilities &nd
larly the British Commonwealth.
2
(e) Extension of Japanese whaling will establish a precedent,
S
whatever might be said now, and subsequent restriction
1
of Japanese activities would be difficult. Japan in
4
the past had ueb a bad record in whaling and completely
f
Rev.
6
Cat.

=== Page 367 === (confidence 1.00)
-2-
failed to observe international conventions designed
to safeguard the future of the industry Australia
consequently regards it of the highest importance that
no action be taken by the Supreme Commander, without
consultation with other powers, which would prejudice
a decision of the future of the Japanese whaling
industry.
2.
On 23 August the Supreme Commander issued a directive
to the Japanese Government ordering the conversion of a
tanker into a whale processing ship. Conversion will not be
completed in time for the present whaling season. It is
clear, from this, that it is not the intention of the Supreme
Commander to confine the rehabilitation of Japanese whaling
to the 1946/47 season. The Australian Government, accordingly,
requests:
(a) The cancellation without delay of the Supreme Commander's
order of 23 August.
(b) The issue of instructions to the Supreme Commander
that no further decision affecting whaling be taken
without prior consultation with the Australian and
other interested Governments.
3.
The Australian Government further proposes that the
1946/47 operations should be an Allied expedition completely
manned by Allied crews. Australia is able to provide a sub-
stantial proportion of the crews and believes that the United
Kingdom, New Zealand, and Norway could provide the rest if
necessary. It is understood that the vessels now available
in Japan can be adapted to use by non-Japanese crews. The
foregoing arrangements should be confined to the one season
1946/47.

=== Page 368 === (confidence 0.97)
-3-
4.
The Australian Government desires that early considera-
tion be given to the prohibition of Japanese whaling in the
Antarctic, at least for the duration of the occupation. It
also considers that Japanese whaling facilities should be
considered not only for reparations but also as part of the
peace settlement.

Memorandum of Conversation
DATE: 9-17-46
SUBJECT:
Question of Japanese participation in the whaling
operations in the Antarctic Ocean during the coming
season.
PARTICIPANTS:
Mr. J. K. Penfield, Deputy Director,

894.628/9-1746
Mr. Knut Lykke, Counselor, Norwegian Embassy.

1-1493
Mr. Lykke called this afternoon at his request and
handed me the attached note which expresses "the categorical
protest of my Government against the sending out of the
whaling expeditions planned by the Supreme Command of the
Allied Powers". I explained to Mr. Lykke that the policiew
under which SCAP operates are laid down by the Far Eastern
Commission and this matter had been brought up in the
Commission. Mr. Lykke said that his Embassy had discussed
the matter with the British but that the latter appeared
reluctant "for political reasons" to bring up the matter
in the Commission.
CS
I questioned the validity of one of the Norwegian
objections to the proposed expeditions; namely, that the
Japanese had in the past not observed international whaling
A
regulations. In replying, Mr. Lykke gave me the definite
impression that the Norwegians are not particularly concerned
over the presently planned expeditions per se. But despite
assurances that this is to be a "one-time operation,"
Wr. Lykke seemed greatly disturbed at the precedent which
would be set. He was also disturbed over the fact thaty
the proposed expeditions will bring together Japanese 
F
whaling personnel and will afford an opportunity totrain
6
new personnel who will, in the future, constitute danger
16
even
5

=== Page 370 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
even if Japanese whaling is prohibited, as they might be
available for hire by the Soviets or other non-whaling
powers who wish to enter this field.
I told Mr. Lykke that we would give prompt attention
to the note.
Attachment:
As stated.

=== Page 375 === (confidence 0.98)
-2
expedition wes an administrative one, quite within the
scope of his powers, and taken as the result of a
d1rective 1gsued in November 1945, by the United States
Government concerning the conduct of Japanese aquatio
industries during the occupation of the country. This
expelition was approved definitely es an emergeney
measure and for one season only in the hope that it
vould help to alleviete the critical world shortage of
fats an? o1ls and thet it would also contribute to
the food supply of Japan. This latter factor is of
particular importance because of the relationship of
the food shortage in Japan to the problems of the
occupation. In this conn ction it is pointed out
that ell of the whale oil produced by the expedition
will be subject to allocation by the International
Emergency Food Council.
It is the intention of the United States Government
and it has been so ordered in directives issued to the
Japanese Government by the Supreme Commander, that the
proposed

=== Page 376 === (confidence 0.99)
proposed whaling expedition be earried out in full
conformity with all the international conventions
and regulations pertaining to whaling. Personnel frem
the Supreme Commander's headquarters will accompany
the expedition to ensure compliance with all such
international regulations.
The suggestion has been made by various interested
governments that it would be possible to convert the
ships to be used on the expedition so that they might
be used by non-Japanese crews and that the entire crews
should be composed of nationals from the several Allied
countries. In an effort to explore every possibility
of meeting the wishes expressed by the interested gov-
ernments a message was sent to the Supreme Commander
requesting his comments on this specifio proposal. In
reply he stated thst it would be entirely impracticable
to endeavor to arrange accommodations for Allied crews
or to use such orews on the expedition. The Supreme
Commander stated that not only would it be economically
unsound

=== Page 377 === (confidence 0.98)
unsound to attempt reconditioning of the vessels, but
it would probably cause suoh delay as to render the
expedition 1mposeible. It was pointed out that the
most serious and perhaps insurmountable diffioulties
could be anticipated in endeavoring to operate and
control such en expedition with crews of mixed nation-
alties. There would inevitably arise the question of
divided responsibility as well as the conflict of
shippin la's of various nationalities. The Supreme
Commender has stated that he could not assume respon-
sibility for the control of mixed Allied crews on an
expedition of this nature and he does not believe that
the proposal could, as a practical matter, be carried
into effect. However, the Supreme Commander has stated
he would welcome the assignment of Allied inspeotors
should any of the nations concerned wish to offer the
services of such persons. In view of the oharacter
of the factory ships to be used, the maximum number of
All1ed personnel possible to accommodate on each ship
w111 be

=== Page 378 === (confidence 0.98)
-5
will be three. This will provide for two Allied
inspectors on eaoh factory ship in addition to the
one inspector who will be assigned from she headquarters
of the Supreme Commander.
The Supreme Commander has stated that the proposed
expedition 1s an Allied expedition under complete Allied
control, that it will be administered by the Supreme
Commander and that it will not fly the Japanese flag.
The ships will fly a special pennant identifiable as
follows: SCAJAP flag 4 feet 6 inohes wide by 5 feet
7 inches long, divided diagonally into two right angle
triangles from upper left to lower right corners with
lower triangle green and upper triangle red. The
SCAJAP flag resembles the international flag "O" with
the exception that the lower triangle on the SCAJAP
flag is green instead of yellow as on the international
flag "o".
The United States Government wishes to emphasize
the mmergenoy charaoter of the proposed expedition and
that its authorization in no way predetermines the
future

-8
Future of Japanese whaling operations. This Governmemt
is in complete accord with the view that any leng-range

ing operations 1s a matter for Allied disoussion and
decision. It is also agreed that the ultimate disposi-
tion of any Japanese whaling vessels or other equipment
1s a subject for inter-Allied consultation and decisien
in connection with reparations from Japan.
In vlew of the facte, as etated above, that this
projected expedition is of an emergency character to
meet a special need, that it will be conduoted in full
conformity with all international whaling agreements
and reguletions, thet it is under Allied supervision
and is accompanied by Allied personnel and that it is
clearly understood the long-term future of Japanese
whaling is a matter for Allied decision, it is the
hope of the United States Government that the Norwegian
Government may be reassured and see fit to withdraw
their objeotions.

6
"The New Zealand Government hes been led to believe, through
-2
press reports and other sources, that the Supreme Commander for
the Allied Powers in Japan has issued a directive authorizing the
sending to the Antarctic for the 1946-47 whaling season of a
46
Japanese whaliug fleet of 12 whalers, 2 factory ships and 7 carriers.

instructed to operate is naturally of reat importance to New

Zealend is the controlling authority.
"The past record of the Japanese in respect of whaling in the
55
Antarctic is one of failure to accede to the International
/V
Conventions regarding the taking of whales, and of predatory
expeditions which threatened the continued existence of the
whaling industry and of the whale itself.
"The operation cf Japanese vessels in such an area will also
raise security questions for New Zealand, the details of which it
DISTRIBUTION
DESIRED
is not necessary to mention at the moment, but which the New Zealand
(OFFICES ONLY)
Government view with some apprehension.
"The New Zealand Government therefore dislikes the prospect of the
resumption, even temporarily, of Japanese whaling activities in
Antarctic waters, and it considers it most important that no action
should be taken which might prejudice sny ultimate policy decision
concerning Japanese re-admission to, or exclusion from, the
Antarctic whaling industry. If, as now seems to be the case,
Japan does in fact appear to have whaling facilities available for
operation, it is possible that the difficulties might be met by
2
~
some form of Allied operation.
"Finally

Dept. received following Aide-Memoire from the Australian

894.628
"On 21 August the Australian member of the Far Eastern Commission
made informal representations to the Director of the Office of Far
Eastern Affairs along the following lines in regard to the exten-
sion of Japanese whaling into the Antarctic:
(a) Australia is greatly concerned at the proposal for the
3
extension of Japanese whaling into waters of direct and
permanent security interest to Australia, without prior
5
consultation with interested governments in accordance with
1
the policy laid down by United States and circulated to the
2
Far Eastern Commission as FEC-C35.
546
(b) The matter appears to be one on which the Allied Council
for Japan should have been consulted and asked to advise.
(c) Approval by the United States Government of the Supreme
Commander's action seems contrary to the spirit of the fourth
C
resolution of the Final Act of the International Whaling
Conference of November 1945.
A
DISTRIBUTION
(a) Australia considers that any whaling facilities and equip
DESIRED
ment in the hands of the Japanese should be made available for
(OFFICES ONLY)
operation by Allied countries, particularly the British Common-
(9
wealth.
(e) Extension of Japanese whaling will establish a precedent,
whatever might be said now, and subsequent restriction of
Japalese activities would be difficult. Japan in the past had
a bad record in whaling and completely failed to observe
international conventions designed to safeguard the future of
the industry. Australia consequently regards it of the highest
importance that no action be taken by the Supreme Commander,
without consul tation with other powers, which would prejudice
a decision

a decision of the future of the Japanesc whaling industry.
"On 23 August the Supreme Commander issued a directive to the
Japanese Government ordering the conversion of a tanker into a whale
processing ship. Conversior will not be completed in time for the
present whaling season. It is clear, from this, that it is not the
intention of the Supreme Commander to confine the rehabilitation of
Jepanese whaling to the 1946/47 season. The Australian Government,
accordingly, requests:
(a) The cancellation without delay of the Supreme Commanderts
order of 23 August.
(b) The issue of instructions to the Supreme Commander that
no further decision affecting whaling be taken without prior
consultation with the Australian and other interested
Governments.
"The Australian Government further proposes that the 1946/47
operations should be an Allied expedition completely manned by
Allied crews. Australia is able to provide a substantial proportion
of the crews and believes that the United Kingdom, New Zealand, and
Norway could provide the rest if necessary. It is understood that
the vessels now available in Japan can be adapted to use by non-
Japanese crews. The foregoing arrangements should be confined to
the one season 1946/47.
"The Australian Government desires that early consideration be
DISTRIBUTION

DESIRED
least for the duration of the occupation. It also considers that
(OFFICES ONLY)
Japanese whaling facilities should be considered not only for
reparations but also as part of the peace settlement.

zile.
AIDE-MEMOIRE
The Department of State has studied carefully
K
the Aide-Memoire on the subjeot of Japanese whaling
-894.628/9-2646
in the Anterotio region left with the Department on
September 16, 1946, by the Counselor of the Austral-
1an Embnsoy on behalf of the Ambassador. The natural
concern of the Australian Government at the proposed
extension of Japanese whaling ectivities into the
waters of the Antarotic is eppreciated, but it 1s the
CS/A
opinion of the Department of State that much of this
concern 1s based upon a misconception as to the pur-
poses of the proposed expedition nd the manner in
which it will be carried out.
The Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers'
authorization of the expedition was a matter of ad-
894.62
ministrative action taken by him under Direotive No. 20A of
November
V
2640

=== Page 395 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
November 13, 1945, which has been filed with the Far
Eastern Commission in accordance with paragraph III, 4,
of its terms of reference and is also contained in
FE0-035. Inasmuch as paragraph d(1) of this direetive
provides that Japanese fishing operations should conform
striotly to "The provisions of agreements relating to
whaling to whioh the United States is a party", the
possibility of the Japanese being permitted to engage
in whaling under the Supreme Commander's authorization
was definitely foreseen and as no member of the Far
Eastern Commission had requested the review of this
direotive as provided for in paragraph II A,2 of the
terms of reference, the action of the Supreme Commander
in approving the forthcoming expedition was olearly
within his authority. This Japanese whaling expedi-
tion was approved by the Supreme Commander particularly
in the hope that it would help to alleviate the oritioal
world shortage of fats and oils and that it would also
oontribute to the food supplies of Japan where, as
dietinct from the majority of nations, whale meat is
an accepted portion of the diet. This latter factor
1

=== Page 396 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
1s of partioular importance because of the relation-
ship of the food shortage to the problems of the
occupation. It is of course understood shat all
whale oil produced by this expedition, not merely
the exportable surplus, will be subjeot to allosa-
tion by the International Emergenoy Food Counoll.
It 1s the intention of the United States Govern-
ment and it hae been so ordered in direotives issued
to the Japanese Government by the Supreme Commander
that the proposed whaling expedition be oarried out
in full conformity with all the international con-
ventions and regulations pertaining to whaling. All
the pertinent treaties, conventions and protocols
governing the conduot of whaling operations in the
Antarotio have been furnished the Japanese Government
and the Department of State has been informed by the
Supreme Commander that in order to insure that these
regulations are carried out in their entirety, Amerioan
inspeotors will be assigned to the two fastory ships.
In eddition there will be Japanese inspeetors appointed
by

=== Page 397 === (confidence 0.99)
the Japanese Government on direction from the Supreme
Commander.
With reference to the fourth resolution of the
Final Aot of the International Whaling Conferenee of
November, 1945, it 1s the recolleotion of members of
the United States delegation to that Conferenee that
this resolution was a recommendation to the oontraet-
ing governments with the purpose and intent of urging
restraint in faoilitating the outfitting and equipping
of wheling expeditions which would not operate in con-
formity with the rules and standards set forth in the
verious whaling agreements. In view of the faot, as
stated above, that the Japanese expedition will be op-
erated in strict conformity with all such international
agreements, it is the belief of the Department of State
that the spirit of the resolution mentioned is not in
any way violated.
The question of security raised in the memorendum
of the Austrelian Embassy 1s one whioh has been care-
fully coneidered both by the Department of State and
by

=== Page 398 === (confidence 0.97)
- 5-
the Supreme Commander. The United States Government
has been informed by the Supreme Commander that all
Japanese personnel on the expedition will be carefully
screened by intelligence offioers before being allowed
to embark. Furthermore, in the directive issued to the
Japanese Government by the Supreme Commander, Japanese
whaling vessels are prohibited from approaching aloser
than 1? miles to any land beyond the home 1slands.
The northern boundary of the area in whiah whaling 1s
authorized is several hundred miles distant from the
neerest metropolitan areas of Australia, Tasmania and
New Zecland. Under the Supreme Commander' e direetive
the Japenese Government is required to furnish him
fifteen days prior to the departure of the expedition,
the route which it is proposed to take to the whaling
grounds. This information will be furnished the
Australian Government and if it should desire to pro-
vide security safeguards in addition to those provided
by the Supreme Commander while the vessels are passing
Australian territory at a distance greater than 12 miles,
the

=== Page 399 === (confidence 0.98)
- 6 -
the recommendations of the Australian Government will
be weloome.
These restriotions on the expedition were design-
ed by the Supreme Commander to keep the Japanese from
going "near areas under allied jurisdiotion" within
the meening of the terms used in Direotive No. 20A.
It was for this reason that oonsultations were not
believed to be required. However, in view of the strong
interest expressed by the Australian Government in this
matter, the Department has been informed by the Supreme
Commander that no authorization for any possible future
Japanese whaling expeditions in the Antarotio or else-
where will be given without prior coneultation with the
Australian and other interested Governments regarding
the seourity faotore that may be involved.
The attention of the Supreme Commander has been
called to the direotive of August 23 authorizing the
conversion of a tenker whioh will not be completed be-
fore the spring of 1947. The Department has been in-
formed by him that permission was granted for the
oonversion

=== Page 400 === (confidence 0.97)
一7一
coavarsion of this vessel but that it has not been
authorized to whale in the Antaretie and no commit-
ment has been made as to its future use for this
purpose or its exemption from reparations removals.
In this oonnection the United States Government
wishes to point out that the ultimate disposition of
Japanese whaling facilities and equipment as well as
the questlon of the future of the Japanese whaling
industry is a matter for Allied consultation and de-
oision. The present authorization is only temporary
and the Japanese have been advised that any equipment
they may possess, whether then available or in prooess
of construotion, is subject to suoh disposition as may
be determined by the Allied Powers. Nor does this
authorization in any way commit the United States
Government to support future whaling operations by the
Japanese.
The opinion has been expressed by the Supreme
Commander that, in view of the unusually small size
of quarters, technical engineering peouliarities and
because their equipment is not of the type ordinarily

=== Page 401 === (confidence 0.97)
-8 -
used by Europeans, it would be inefficient to operate
the ships to be used on the expedition by other than
Japanese crows. It is also believed that it would be
impractioal to attempt to convert these ships for
European use in the time available before the expedi-
tion must set out.
The Government of the United States wishes to
emphasize the purely temporary character of this pro-
jeeted expedition and its emergency nature in conneo-
tion with the food supply situation in Japan and
throughout the world. This Government is in oomplete
agreement with the view that any long-range considera-
tion of the rights of Japan to conduot whaling operations
1s a matter for Allied disoussion and decision.
In view of the temporary and emergenoy charaoter
of this proposed expedition whioh in no way constitutes
a precedent for the future, because of the faot that
this expedition will be carried out in full and stries
conformity with all international agreements regerding
whaling, because adequate security safeguards will be
established

=== Page 402 === (confidence 0.89)
established and as arrangements have been made to en-
sure prior consultation with the interested Governments,
inoluding the Government of Australia, in conneetion
with any proposals for future Japanese whaling expedi-
tione, it is hoped that the conoern of the Australian
Government may have been assuaged.
Department of State,

Investi tion Division of the China Theater. It is understood
here that this report was subse mently forwarded to the State
Depart unt where photost tie copies were made and that one set of
photostatic copies has been sent to ir. Arnold of the small Har
Ilembs Sorporation.
2. The Jud e Advecate leneral of the Army has advised that
Ir. Golistone is not presently subject to military law and has
reconmended tit the pearls contiscated in China be returned to
his agent in China. Since no charges have been brought against
r. toldstone b either the state Department or small War Plants
Corporation, it is the opinion of the ar Department that continued
retention of the pearls by the Army is not legally justified.
3. Therefore, the ar Department is forwarding to Ir.
Goldstone a release form to insure that neither the U.3. Government
nor any individual thereof will be subject to retaliatory legal
action by virtue of having confiscated the pearls. Upon receipt
of the signed release form, instructions will be issued to the
Connanding General, China Theater to surrender the confiscated
pearls to Ir. Goldstone's duly authorized agent in Shanghai, China.

SUBJECT: Howard H. MACGOWAN and Joseph GOLDSTO, Alleged Repre-
sentatives of United States Senate Small Business Committee
TO
: Connanding General, United States Forces, China Theater,
APO 879.
SUMLARY:
How rd H. MacGowan and Joseph Goldstone,arrived in Shang-
hai on or about 2? November 1s45, allegedly as representatives of
the Unitec States Senate Small Business Committee, for the purpose
of making an official survey of business conditions and opportuni-
ties for small business in Crina. Immediately after their arrival
and throughout their stay in China they conducted negotiations for
large scale private business trensactions with the very apparent
purpose of effecting as much of a monopoly of the available worla
supply of cultured pearls as was possible. Although possessing no
specific authority for such purpose, these subjects also made & six
dey tri to Jap:n in connection with the cultured pearl industry
there.
Mr. Goldstone was the principal in these transactions, al-
though MacGowan admittedly had full knowledge of and was present at
most of the negotitions. r. Goldstone represents one of the larg-
est American pearl concerns and brought with him to China approximately
$120,000.00 in currency, checks anc letters of credit, and also ar-
ranged for additio.u1 financing in the sum of $300,000.00. Mr. Gold-
stone attempted to lee ve China by air on 17 December 1945 with a large
siipment of pearls. His trip W's cancelled and the pearls seized.
This investigation was €onducted with the concurrence and
approval of Mr. Maurey Maverick, General Manager, Small War Plants
Corporation, a United States governaental agency, who strongly con-
demned subject's practices of concucting private business for profit
While allegedly engag-d on official business and travelling under
government auspices; the American Consulate and the Commanding Gen-
eral, China Theater.

transactions on a large scale with the apparent purpose of effecting
a monopoly of the available world supply of cultured pearls.
2. Investigation of these reports Was imnediately insti-
tuted since the illegal use of governnent transportation was involved.

I. There is some doubt as to the exist nce of proper authority
for subjects' trip to China and later to Japan. Such credentials as have
been seen do not specifically authorize the trips und fail to indicate the

was authorized there seems no doubt that its purpose was cbused and corrupted
into a venture for private cain. There vas no atte apt on the part of either
of these "representatives of the Senate" to cover any part of China but

To Com Gen. China Division
Crim. Inv. Division
Gentleman:
In so far as I am concerned, you are not only authorized, but re-
quested to take such (steps) as are necessary in the Goldstone - McGowan
affair. In my opinion they should be fully investigated in a manner that
will lead to a full study of the case.
No request for eir priority for Mr. McGowan is made as ship trans-
portation will be sufficient if the seme is helpful to the investigation
and in any event there is no urgent need of Mr. McGowan's service.
As for Goldstone, I do not know him. but if he in any way is taking
advantage of the authority or courtesies of the Senate, it is my opinion
such body will disapprove of such acts. Therefor Goldstone should be afforded
such protective measures as are necessary to insure the integrity of the
Senate as well as his own.
Please take whatever action is necessary.
/s/ Maurey Maverick

=== Page 435 === (confidence 0.98)
 STATEMENT OF: Joe Goldstone,
10 West Elm Street,
CHICAGO, 111.
I was appointed Far Eastern consultant by Senator James E. Murray.
who is Chairman of a special Committee to study problems of American
small business. Senator Lurray's office took care of the transportation
and arrangements to come to China and Japan. I was travelling with
Howard McGowan, who also represented this Committee. The purpose of the
trip was to promote business between small business concerns in America
and China. cGowan and I left the United States on 16 Nov 45 from Wa-
shington. We travelled by ATC to unming and then to Shanghai. We arrived
in Shanghai on about 22 Nov 45. While in Shanghai we interviewed many
business concerns with reference to promoting trade between Chinese and
American business men. We remained in Shanghai about ten (1o) days and
then went to Tokyo. ie stayed in Japan for six (6) days. During the
time we were in Japan we completed the mission we were sent for and
returned to Shangha1 about 9 Dec 45.
While I was in Shanghai I purchased certain cultured pearl necklaces
from the Cathay Jewelry in the sum or US$ 2oooo.- for the Imperial Pearl
Syndicate of' which firm I am a member. I filed a Custom's Declaration
for these pearls and paid the Chinese Import and Export Duty and also
recelved a Consular Invoice from the American Consulate for same and
declared them as baggage and paid the air-express charges on same from
Shanghai to Washington D.C. by ATC.
Prior to leaving Washington I ascertained from the ATC whether
air-express was permissable and I was told that it was, if it was paid
for.
Another purchase was made from the Cathay Jewelry to be shipped by
boat later on in the sum of US$ o500.- for which I paid. I had also
paia for the US$ 20000.- shipment or pearls which were being sent by
air-express. A tentative offer was made to buy pearls at approximately
US$ 50u0.- to US$ 6000.- which transaction was to be confirmed from the
States. This last transaction was with a Mir. David.
Apart from the purchase or the S$ 2oooo.- worth of pearls and the
US 85o0.- worth of pearls and the tentative arrangements concerning the
US$ 5000.- to US$ 6000.- worth of pearls, I purchased no other merchan-
dise or any description whatsoever, except some gifts. I did not enter
into any transactions other than the ones spccified above,
I have a letter from Senator Jemes E. Murray, Chairman, U.S. Senate
Special Committee to study problems of American Small Business dated

This letter reads:
"My dear Mr. Goldstone; Please accept my sincere thanks
for your consent to act as a consultant to the Senate Small
Business Committee in connection with its studies on Foreign
trade in the Far East. I am very glad that you will find
time to devote to the problems confronting my Committee, and
I wish at this time to formally designate you as Consultant
of Foreign trade to the Far East.-
I am looking forward to assigning important studies to you.
Sincerely yours,
S/ James E. Murray."
The transactions for the purchase of pearls which I have refered
to above were for the account of the Imperial Pearl Syndicate and were
made solely by myself without the assistance of Ir. McGowan who has no
interest whatsoever in these purchases. When I was authorized to proceed
to the Far East as a representative of the Senate Small Business Commit-
tee, it was agreed and understood that I was to pay all my own expenses
on this trip. I have paid all transportation costs and all living ex-
penses which I incurred on this trip.
It was my understanding when I left Washington that if the occasion
presented itself I could transact private business for myself.

Statement regarding conference on 18 December 1945 at Office of
Criminal Investigation Division, Development Building. Shanghai,
China.
Present:
Howard H. MacGowan
Joseph Goldstone
Lt. Col. John F. Stoskopf, Jr.
Lt. George Plotkin
At about 1000 hours on 18 December 1945. Mr. Howard H. MacGowan
and Mr. Joseph Goldstone, toth of whom are in China allegedly on an offi-
cial mission for the United States Senate Small Business Committee, call-
ed at the office of the Criminal Investigation Division, Office of the
Theater Provost Marsial, Head uarters, United States Forces, China Theater,
which is located on the fourtetnth floor of the Development Building.
Shanghai, China. They were received there by Lt. George Plotkin, Assistant
Director, Criminal Investigation Division.
Mr. MacGowan and Mr. Goldstone were informed that the Criminal
Investigation Division was conductine an investigation of charges that Mr.
Golastone had attempted to use government transportation for carrying mer-
chandise out ofthe China Theater for comnercial purposes, which is in vio-
lation of existing regulations in this Theater, and trat 'r. MacGowan
and/or Mr. Goldstone had been transactin& private business for profit while
engaged on official sovernment business and travelling under official gov-
ernment unspices.
ar. kacGowan was also inforned that he need make no statement,
written or orel, nor need ne unswer any question, nor volunteer any in-
foraation unless he desired io do so willingly and witlout promise of any
inducenent or reward. r. G)lastone haa been similarly advised on the
previous day by Lt. Col.O'Connor of the office of the Theater Judge Advo-
cate Gereral.
Mr. MacGowan was then usked whether he had engaged in any private
business transactions while in China. This he categorically denied.
Mr. MacGowan was ssked if he knew whether Mr. Goldstone had en-
gaged in any private business transactions while in China. Mr. MacGowan
stated that he knew thnt ir. Goldstone had purchased a guantity of cul-
tured pearls during their stey in Shanghai.
Mr. MacGowan further admitted that he had been present when Mr.
Goldstone had negotituted for the purchase of pearls with different dealers
in Snanghai, but stated that he had no personal interest in such transac-
tions.
Mr. MacGowan was then asked whetner he considered it proper for
Mr. Golastone to make such purchases while they were engnged on an official
governmental mission. Mr. MacGowan stated that he had not been told that
such practice was improper of forbidden and that ke did not see anything
wrong with it if it did not interfere with or prevent the completion of
their mission.

Mr. MacGowan and Mr. Goldstone were asked whether either or

Mr. Goldstone was asked to state how much money he had brought
with him to China, whether in the form of currency, checks, credits, or
documents. This question he refused to answer.
At this point Lt. Col. Stoshopf arrived and joined the confer-

written statement whatsoever.

specified in his statement, namely the murchase of a total of approximately
$28,500.00 vorth of pearls from Catnay Jewellery, and negotiations for

the only transactions he had conducted.
Mr. Goldstone was asked for the full name and address of "Mr.
Davia". He stated that it was a small shop in the vicinity and that he
would give the exact nane and address later.
Er. MacGowan remarked in effect several times during the course
of this conference thatthe subject investigation was in the nature of a
"fishing expedition" in an effort to discredit himself and the Senate
Committee which he represented. Mr. Golastone also repeated his former

Before the war broke out Mr. Goldstone requested that a shipment of

assembled a lot of pearls and Mr. Golastone had already forwarded the
money for payment to a local bank, when the war started and we could not

I did not take part in all ofthe business negotiations between them

Statement of AATOI BEERBRAYR, 241 Cardinal Mercier, Shanghai, China.
I am a wholesale dealer in gens, particularly in cultured pearls. One
of the dealers to whom I sell is Mr. . Lipkovsky, owner ofthe Cathay Jewellery.
About the middle of November 1g45. Mr. Lipkovsky told me that a dealer was
coming over from the United States to buy pearls. Mr. Lipkovsky asked me to
give him some sort of option an my entire stock for this dealer, and I agreed
to hold it for a few days until he could get some instructions.
About ten days later, near the end of the month, Mr. Lipkovsky called
to tell me that the cealer fed arrived and he arranged to bring him over to
look at my stock of pearls. He came over witn Mr. Joreh Goldstone, who he
introduced to me as being the biggest pearl dealer in the United States, end
Mr. Howard NacGowan, who he told me was a member of the United States Senate
and a very important man.
Mr. Goldstone told me that he wanted to buy my entire stock of pearls be-
cause he wanted to corner the whole market on pearls. Ee showed me two letters
of credit for $50,000.00 each and traveller's checks for about $20,000.00.
After we agreed on the prices we found that my stock of pearls was worth
about $400, O00.00. Mr. Goldstone told me that he could get all the money he
wantec fron tne United States. I told him that I would have to have payment
in cash in U.S. dollars.
On 2 December 1g45, I gave Mr. Goldstone a written option to purchase my
stock of pearls consisting of approximately two hunared ten thousand momme (
over 1900 pounas) of loose pear1s at $1.50 per momme and about 5693 pear1 neck-
laces at $1a.00 each. The option was to expire on 10 December 1945. This was
to let Mr. Golcston: have enough time to cable to the United States to make
arrangements for payment. I have turned over my copy of this agreement to Lt.
George Plotkin, United States Arny Criminal Investigation Division, after he
and I had merked it with our initials for identifåcation.
Mr. Goldstone luter showed me & cable from his bank in Chicago saying
that they woula advance $300, 000.00 ageinst the merchandise. I said that this
would be satisfactory to me and that I would accept payment in the United
States upon delivery of shipping documents to the bank there.
Then we found that Mr. Goldstone could not cash his letters of credit in
Shanghai because of restrictions put on the banks by the Chinese Government.
He wanted me to forward the pe rls without any payment here but I refused to
do this ana the option expired.
I saw Mr. Goldstone and Mr. MacGowen concerning this transaction about
six times altogether. Mr. Gokdstone cid all the talking and arranging and
Mr. MacGowan seemed to be a sort of silent partner.
I understood that Mr. Goldstone and Mr. MacGowan went to Japan also to
find out about the pearl industry there. I do not know whether they did any
business there, but I understand that at the present time nothing can be ex-
ported out of Japan.

taken note of verbal assurances iven subsequently that the ships of the expedi-
tion would not be allowed to fly the Japanese flag, and that consultation would
take place before all subsequent epeditione in the Antarctic or elsewhere and
woulu not be confined to security factors.
The Australian vovernnent reatly regrets that the Government of the
United states has not agreed that the expedition should be nanned solely by Allied
seanen and that no Japanese chould be allowed to serve. The Australian Government
is in a position te pr vide a substantial proportion cf the crews in all categories
except possibly munners on chaser ships. It has also been advised, contrary to
the opinion expressed in the united otates side mémoire, that the Japanese vessels
94.
could oe adapted to use y non-Japanese crews, particularly if reconditioning
were em irmediately. It is therefore requested that the decision on this p ointo
2

the entire crews of the expedition.
The Australian voverment desires to reneat its regret that a Japanese
expedition should have cen cuthorized, without prior consultation, to op rate in
waters of such irect concern to Australia. It also fears that, whatever might
be said to the contrary, the expedition mi ht be taken as a precedent for the re-
habilitation of the Japanese whalin industry, which would be undesirable in view
CS/HH
of the bad Japanese record in this regard before the war. Irrespective of the
particinntion of Allied crews, it is desired that it sheuld be made clear beyond

international discussions.

by the Japanese. However, in view of the foregoing, it is hoped that the Govern-
ment of the united states will feel able to reconsider its position in regard
to the forthcomin expedition.

AIDE-MÉMOIRE
The Government of the United States has considered
carefully and sympathetically the aide-mémoire of
October 5, 1946, concerning the proposed whaling
expedition to the Antarctic from Japan, left with the
Acting Secretary.of State by the Australian Ambassador.
894.628/10-546
In an endeavor to explore all pogsibilities and meet
as far as possible the wish of the Australian Government
that the interested Allied governments be permitted to
supply the entire crews of the expedition, a message
was sent to the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers
in Tokyo requesting his further comments as to the
CS/HH
possibility of acceding to the Australian request.
A reply has now been received from the Supreme
Commander stating that it would be entirely impractical
to endeavor to arrange accommodations for Allied crews
894.628/0
or to
2908

=== Page 465 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
or to utilize such crews on the forthooming expoditiom.
It is pointed out that to attempt reconditioning the
vessels for use by Allied orews would not only be
economically unsound but would probably cause sueh delay
as to render the expedition impossible. In the opinion
of the Supreme Commander the most serious difriculty in
carrying out the Australian proposal would be in attempt-
ing to control and operate such an expedition with orews
of mixed nationalities. There would inevitably arise
the question of divided responsibility as well as the
problems raised by the conflict of shipping laws of the
various nationalities. In addition to the above diffi-
culties there would be those in connection with housing
and feeding the crews pending sailing and in insuring
uniform pay, rations and equipment. The Supreme Com-
mander states that he could not accept the responsibility
for the control of mixed Allied crews on such an expedi-
tion and that he does not believe that the proposal could,
as a practical matter, be carried into effect,
However,

=== Page 466 === (confidence 0.99)
-3
However, the Supreme Cormander has stated that he
would heartily welcome additional Allied inspectors to
the forthcoming whaling expedition should any of the
neticns concerned wish to offer the servisss of such per-
sons. Because of the meager quarters it will only be
possible to accormodate three Allied personnel on eseh
factory ship and the Supreme Conmander has stated 1t
would therefore be possible for two Allied inspectors to
be assigned to each ship in addition to the inspector
fror his headquarters. The Supreme Commander desires to
point ot that acco modations and meals will be sub-
standard and that Allied inspectors should be prepared
for ccnsiderable hardship. Inforwetion as early as
possible is requested by the Supreme Commander if any
of the nations concerned desire to appoint inspectors
together ith the dete when they may be expected to
report to the Fisheries Division, Hatural Resourees See-
tion, of his headquarters. It is understocd that the
foreign governments concerned will pay the salaries and
provide

=== Page 467 === (confidence 0.99)
provide the necessary equipment and rations of such
inspectors as they may assign for duty with the expedi-
tion. Inasmueh as it is believed advisable to have
equality of pay for all foreign inspectors, the Supreme
Commander has requested that he be notified as soon as
possible as to the salary it is contemplated paying any
inspectors who may be appointed.
As vas stated in this Government's aide fimoire of
September 26, 1946, the forthcoming whaling expedition
is an emergency one, for one season only and is under the
complete control of the Supreme Commander who has stated
that in his opinion it should be considered as an Allied
expedition. As the Australian Government is aware the
expedition will not fly the Japanese flag but a special
SCAJAP pennent. This pennent is the same that is flown
by all ships operatin, under the control of the Supreme
Commander in the Pacific area and may be seen on all
American ships, many with Japanese officers and crews,
which are engaged in the repatriation of Japanese
civilians

=== Page 468 === (confidence 0.97)
-5
civilians and military personnel to the home islands.
All such ships are under the control of the naval seetiom
of the Supreme Headquarters and under the direetion of
Rear Admiral Momsen of the United States Navy. The SCAJAP
flag is identifiable as follows: 4 feet 6 inches wide by
5 feet 7 inches long, divided diagonally into two right
angle triangles from upper left to lower right corners
with lower triangle green and upper triangle red. The
SCAJAP flag resembles the international flag "O" with
the exception that the lower triangle on the CAJAP flag
is green instead of yellow as on the international flag
"C"
This government wishes to confirm again its previous
statement that the question of whether the Japanese shell
or shall not heve a whaling industry in the future is a
matter for Allied consultation and decision. This applies
also to the ultimate disposition of all Japanese whaling
equipment and facilities. In the aide mémoire left by
the Australian Ambassador it 1s stated that the Australian
Govornment

=== Page 469 === (confidence 0.94)
-6
Govermment cannot agree to any recognition of Japanese
whaling rights. It is the opinion of this Govermment
that the proper forum for the discussion of the future
whaling rights of Japan is the Far Fastern Commission
where the whcle question can be discussed at length with
a view to the establishment of a definite policy aceept-
able to all.
In view of the above considerations the Government
of the United states regrets that it cannot accede to
the request of the Australian Government that the
entire crew of the fortheoming expedition be composed
of nationals of the Allied countries.

Major Plimsoll called to deliver the attached aide-mémoire

C

6/A

is flown at one end of the ships and the modified international
code flag "E" is flown at the other end, which if true would
make it difficult to maintain that the Japanese flag is being
used as a component of a special SCAJAP flag.
I told Major Plimsoll that I would make inquiries regarding
this matter and that a reply to the Embassy's communication
894.628

Stotrnnd ly Anliali amlmsodon
MR.CHAIRMAN:
My Government's opposition to the resmption of Japanese
Antarctic whaling is well-known. Is has been stated by Australian
representatives at meetings of this Commission and of its Committees, and
has been pointed out in aide-memoires to the State Department. Australias
position has been unambiguous from the beginning: we are opposed to any
deep-sea whaling by the Japanese before the permanent future of this
industry has been decided by the peace oonference.
This opposition has been based on many grounds. We consider
that the presence of Japanese in Australian or Antarctic waters 
 constitutes a threat to the security and welfare of Australia.
Factory ships are capable of conversion into tankers and submarine
refuelling vessels, and chasers can be converted into naval patrol craft.
We know that during the war former Japanese whaling vessels were used for
naval purposes, and proved of great value to the enemy because they were speck
ially constructed in order to be readily converted into war usese Moreover,
the crews of these ships are given an opportunity to gain valuable experienee
in Antarctic waters and to make scientific obeervations. These things
should not be permitted before the peace conference has had an opportunity
to discuss and determine the whole question of the military disarmament
and control of Japan. I would like to point out, Mr. Chairman, that if
these arguments seem to carry much more weight in Australia and New Zealand
than they apparently do in Washington, it is because both Dominions are
exposed and alone in the South Pacific and are the ones most closely affeoted
by these operations of the Japanese. Ninety per cent. of the whales
captured by the Japanese last year were taken off Australian Antarctio
territories.
The Japanese violations of international whaling conventions in
the past are well known. Their depredations have done much to reduce the
mumbers of whales now living in these seas and this to reduce the quantity
of whale oil available to the world. The Japanese ruthlessly ed all
the whales they could get, regardless of sex or age, regardless of whether
they were with calf or not. A few years of enforced abstinence from

=== Page 478 === (confidence 0.98)
2.
whaling would do no more than allow the Japanese to make some reoompense
to the rest of the world for their past conduct in redueing the total mmber
of whales to its present figure. Moreover, Mr. Chairman, we have me
confidence that the Japanese in future will observe the international
whaling conventions with any better faith than in the past.
Another Japanese expedition to the Antarotie will not inorease
the quantity of oil available to the world this year. The international
whaling conference has limited the total catoh to 16,000 blue whale units.
Exclusive of the Japanese, at least sixteen Allied factory ships will be
operating in 1947/48, and they will be able to attain the maximm of
16,000 whales. Therefore, any Japanese catohes will be at the expense
of Allied vessels. But mot only will the total number of whales captured
be no greater; the total output of oil will probably be amaller. Japanese
whaling methods before the war were most wasteful and unsatisfactory, and
they continued to be so last year. My Government has estimated that, from
the whales which were captured by the Japanese last year, at least 3,000
tons more oil could have been obtained, which at the present price of loo
a ton represents a loss of £300,000, or some $1,000,000. I understand that
the Norwegian Government has made estimates which are even higher. We are
informed that the Supreme Commander intends to take steps to improve the
Jpaanese ships and methods so that this waste will not continue, but we
cannot forget that ships were allowed to leave Japan last year obviously
chei
unfitted for tme job, despite the fact that the Supreme Commander's attention
had been drawn to the dangers of waste. I might add, for the information
of members who might not have already perused it, that I have had
distributed to members, on llth June, a copy of the official report of the
Australian observer who accompanied the last Japanese whaling expedition
to the Antarotic - this is document C2-231/2, and fully bears out what I
have been saying about waste and infringements of the international
conventions.
At this stage, Mr, Chairman, I should like to recapitulate the
course of ovents over the past year. My Government, in common with the
Governments of New Zealand, Norway and the United Kingdom, protested very

=== Page 479 === (confidence 0.99)
3.
strongly in 1946 against the Japanese expedition to the Antarotie last
year. Our protests were of no avail, and the expedition sailed.
However, we did receive certain assurances from the United States
Government, one of which was that the question of the future of the
Japanese whaling industry was a matter of Allied consultation and decision.
We were also assured that the Australian Government would be fully
consulted in connection with any future proposals concerning Japanese
whaling with which the United States was concerned. I understand that
similar assurances were given to the Governmenta of New Zealand, Morway
and the United Kingdom.
Last December my Government introduced a paper into the Far
Eastern Commission - FEC-035/1 - which would have had the offect of
forbidding Japanese whaling in the Antarctic. This paper was carefully
considered in the economic committee, and by the end of February had
secured the support of every country represented on the Commission with
the exception of the United States. At this stage the American member,
finding himself outvoted by 10 to 1, suggested that the committee agree
to divide the paper into two separate papers covering fishing and whaling,
and the committee was led to believe that the United States was preparing
a paper which would have forbidden Japanese Antarctic whaling. On that
understanding the committee adjourned its discussion, and whenever members
raised questions subsequently they were assured that the papers were being
prepared.
Suddenly my Government was confronted on 27th May with a
statement from General Hilldring that a second Japanese expedition was
contemplated, and this was followed swiftly on 9th June with an aide-memoire
bluntly announcing that an expedition would definitely be authorised - a
unilateral act which disregarded the wishes of every other member of this
Commission and every other interested Government. Apparently, Mr.
Chairman, the three monthe' respite which the economic committee gave the
United States Government as a matter of courtesy, resulted, mot in an
American counter-proposal designed to meet the views of other Gevernments,
but in the rest of the Commission being lulled into a sense of false seeurity
and in the ultimate frustration of their wishes.

=== Page 480 === (confidence 0.97)
Burely no one could maintain that the assuranses given to my
Government in 1946 have been fulfilled. There was no real "Allied
consultation and decision." My Government was not "fully consulted in
connection with future proposals concerning Japanese whaling."
Apparently General MacArthur's proposal for a second expedition was
considered in Washington for some weeks before any other Govermment was
informed. If we had been approached frankly in April and informed of
the Sapreme Commander's proposals and the United States views, we g
ae
confidort we could
have reached a detailed agreement acceptabhe to all. My Government
proposed last year that the expodition should be manned by Allied crows,
and assured the United States of Australia'a ability to provide the erew
of one factory ship and ancillary craft. I remember very well an
interview which I had with Mr. Dean Aoheson on the 5th of October, in
which I renewed that offer. But apparently, in all the consideration
given to this latest expedition before other Governments were consulted,
no consid ration was given to the earlier Australian offer to provide crews,
or to the possibility of an Allied expedition.
Immediately my Government learned that a second expedition was
contemplated, we made an earnest endeavour to find a solution which would
not only meet our position but satisfy the United States. The ohief
argument posed by the United States appears as follows in an aide-memoire
to my Government on 9th June of this year: *The protein food products
and whale oil provided by the expedition will be a vitally necessary
component of Japanese food and oil supplies during the calendar year 1948...
If the requisite protein foods and oil for calendar l948 are not obtained
from whaling operations, it will fall upon the United States, which contimues
in the interest and to the advantage of all the Allies to supply the entire
Japanese food deficit, to make up the deficiency, something which, in view
of the manifold demands on its fats and oils resources, it can ill afford to
do."
My Government carefully considered this argument. We are not
unappreciative of the burdens borne by the United States, and on 
many
s occasionsin this Commission and in Japan we have shown a willingness

=== Page 481 === (confidence 0.98)
5.
to co-operate in aharing or relieving those burdens. You mmst remember
that we did not have muoh time to act on this matter. If we had been
consulted last April, we could have discussed various proposals in detail.
But we did what we could. Mr. Mackahon Ball discuesed the position with
General MacArttur and officials of his ataff. Here in Washington last
week we began to discuss a proposal which I can best summarise by quoting
from an aide memoire whioh I have since given to the State Departments
"The diffioulties of the United States Government in finamoing
relief for Japan and the difficulties of the Supreme Commander in securing
adequate supplies are fully appreciated. The Australian Government is
prepared to play its part in relieving these difficulties. To this and
it proposes that a factory ship and chasers should be made available to
Australia immediately for use in the coming whaling season. The Australien
Government is in a position to man these ships and operate them more
efficiently than the Japanese, thus increasing the amount ofal availeble.
The allocation of the food products to relieve the Japanese food shortage
would be entirely a matter for determination by the Supreme Commander, and
the oil would be allocated as at present by the Intermational Food
Emergency Committee, thus assuring supplies at least as adequate as those
which would be obtained from the proposed Japanese expedition. The
Australian Government is prepared to consider methods of payment which would
avoid the cost being a charge on United States revenue. If a separate
Australian expedition is not considered feasible or desirable, Australia
is prepared to conduct the expedition in association with other Allies,
in particular the United Kingdom, New Zealand, and Norway.
In putting forward that proposal, Mr. Chairman, my Government
deliberately refrained from going into detail because we wished to give
full opportunity for the United States to state, and for us to take accoumt
of, the practical difficulties and views of the Supreme Commander and the
United States Government. There are obviously many courses open in giving
effect to our offer, such as allooating a ship as advance reparations
1 deliveries

=== Page 482 === (confidence 0.97)
6.
deliveries or leasing a ship, and there are many weys of considering the
financial problems involved. We feel that details can readily be worked
out if agreement is first reached on the funamental principless that
Japanese Antarotie whaling this year should be forbiddens and that the
Allied themselves should be given all rights of operating any omergeney
expedition for the purpose of obtaining whale meat and oil for the Japanese,
to be allocated by SCAP and the International Emergency Food Committee.
My Governmont feels this is a matter for decision by the Far
Eastern Commission, not for unilateral action by one Government. My
Government's posktion has been supported at Committee level by nine other
Governments, and I hope they will remain firm. I accordingly move the
adoption of FEC-231/4.

=== Page 521 === (confidence 0.99)
Encl No. 1 of Tokyo
Despatch No. 649
Regulation Law of Factory Whaling
Article 40.
For the purpose of operating factory whaling, it is
approved to use only one factory ship in the North Pacific
Ocean north of 200 North Latitude (including the Berling
Sea, the Sea of Okhotsk, and the Arctic Ocean, the same
in the followings).
Article 41.
It is forbidden to operate factory whaling in the
area surrounded by Longitudes 1800 North and 1590 East,
Latitudes 20° North and 52° 30' North.
Article 4l,II It is forbidden to take or kill Grey Whales and/or
Right Whales (including Bowheads).
Article 4l,III It is forbidden to take or kill the following whales.
1. Calves, suckling whales, or female whales which are
accompanied by calves or suckling whales.
Blue whales under 21.34 meters long
3.
Fin whales under 16.77 meters long
4.
Humpback whales under 10.67 meters long
5.
Sei whales under 10.67 meters long
6. Sperm whales under 10.67 meters long
Length in relation to any whale of the preceding paragraph
from No. 1 to No. 6 means the distance measured on the
level in a straight line between the tip of the upper jaw
and notch between the flukes of the tail.
Article 4l,IV. It is forbidden to take or kill baleen whales in any
water south of 4o0 South Latitude except the period from
8th day of December to 7th day of next March.
Article 4l,V. It is forbidden to use the factory ship for the
purpose of treating baleen whales in any other water
during one year after the period prescribed in the pre-
ceding Article which was operated in the water south of

=== Page 522 === (confidence 1.00)
40° South Latitude for the same purpose.
Article 42,
It is forbidden to convert any equipment of a factory
ship used for treating whales, provided that it is per-
mitted by the Minister of Agriculture and Forestry.
Article 42,II. The fullest possible use shall be made of all whales
taken.
The oil shall be extracted by boiling or otherwise
from all blubber, meat, bones, and all parts of whales,
but it is approved not to extract the oil from the follow-
ing:
1. The internal organs, baleen, or fins.
2. Whales or parts of whales intended for human food, .
feeding animals, or other suitable uses.
3. The meat of sperm whales.
Article 42,III An Superintendent of whaling or any one who directs
the business in place of a factory ship should take proper
measures in order not to catch or kill a greater number
of whales than can be delivered on board of the factory
ship within a period of 33 hours from the time of the
killing of each whale.
Article 42,IV. Gunners and crews of factory ships or of whale
catchers attached to them, shall be engaged on terms such
that their remuneration shall depend to a considerable
extent upon such rate as the number, size, species, oil
and yield of whales taken.
No remuneration, calculated by reference to their
result of their work, shall be paid to the gunners and
crews of whale catchers in respect of any whales the
taking of which is forbidden by this regulation.
Any person who operates factory whaling shall make
an account showing the total emolument of each gunner
and member of the crew and the detailed statement in

=== Page 523 === (confidence 1.00)
Encl No. 2 of Tokyo
Despatch No. 649
MINISTRIAL ORDINANCE
Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry
No.
Sept.
1946.
The following temporary reform shall be made in Article 41, No 4
of Regulation Law of Factory Fishing.
Minister of Agriculture and Forestry
Hiroo Wada
Notwithstanding Article 41, No. 4 of the Regulation Law of
Factory Fishing, baleen whales may be taken in any water south of
400 South Latitude during the period from the 6th day of December
1946, to the 7th day of April 1947.
Notwithstanding Article 41, No. 4 and the provision of the
preceding paragraph, humpback whales shall not be taken in the preced-
ing water during the period from the 8th of December 1946, to the 7th
day of April 1947.
This ordinance shall be put in force on and after the day of its
promulgation.

=== Page 524 === (confidence 1.00)
Encl No. 3 of Tokyo
Despatch No. 649
The extracts of the Articles of Penal provisions in
Fisheries Law
Article 35
No steamship trawler fishing, factory ship fishing,
steamship whaling or Danish seine fishing shall be operated
without a permission of the competent Minister in accordance
with orders.
The restriction or prohibitions in reference to the
preceding paragraph are decided by the competent Minister.
Article 59
In case of steamship trawler fishing or factory ship
fishing, anyone who violated the provisions of Par. 1 of
Article 35, or the restrictions or prohibitions of Par. 2 of
the same Art. is subject to a fine of ¥5,000 or less; in case
of steamship whaling or Danish seine fishing, anyone who
violated the provisions of Par. 1 of the same Art., the
restrictions or prohibitions of Par. 2 of the same Art., or
the provisions of the Article 36 shall be subject to a fine
of ¥2,000 or less.
In this case the catch, products and fishing implements
possessed or kept by the offender may be confiscated.
Provided that the stated things possessed by the
offender cannot be confiscated in part or wholly, the price
corresponding to them may be forfeited.
Penal regulations as regards violation of the provi-
sions of Orders (Law No. 84 September 18, 1890)
Any person who violated the provisions of orders shall
be subject to a fine of ¥2oo or less, or to imprisonment for
one year or less in accordance with regulations provided for
the respective order.

=== Page 525 === (confidence 0.99)
Enc1 No. 4 of Tokyo
Despatch No. 649
GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
MEMORANDUM
1. Reference is made to Washington Radio State Serial 500,
dated 27 September, in which State Department requests details re-
garding training of whaling inspectors and information regarding
provisions for penalties for violation of whaling agreements and
ordinances. Information is also desired on provisions being made
for biological investigations, use of the Japanese Merchant
Marine flag, and the reported killing of a right whale during the
1945-46 Bonin Islands Whaling Season.
2. There is no special training system used for training
inspectors of whaling operations. Qualifications of inspectors
are as follows:
a. Inspectors must be graduates of a Fisheries College
or University.
b. Inspectors are usually selected from employees of
the Whaling Section of the Bureau of Fisheries and especially
from those whose work is concerned with supervision of whaling
regulations.
c. Inspectors must be physically fit and be between the
ages of 20 to 50 years.
d. The Japanese Bureau of Fisheries appoints two whaling
inspectors for each factory ship. Therefore, the number of inspec-
tors varies according to the number of factory ships in operation.
The inspectors are appointed to serve for a period of one year but
may be reappointed from year to year. One of the two appointees
must have had previous service aboard a factory ship as a whaling
inspector and must be an "Official Technician Second Class."
(Fisheries Technicians are given ratings commensurate with their
educational background, experience, and length of service with the
Bureau of Fisheries). No official Japanese Government inspection
is provided for whaling land stations.
3. There exists several provisions for penalties for viola-
tions of "Regulations of Whaling Factory Ships" and under the
factory ships section of the Fishery Law. Copies of these laws and
regulations as submitted 9 October 1946 by the Japanese Bureau of
Fisheries are enclosed. In addition, there exists Imperial Ordi-
nance No. 311 dated 11 June 1946, which provides a penalty of
¥ 75,000 or 10 years imprisonment for the violation of a SCAP
directive. Inasmuch as any violations of the International Whaling
Agreement would be in violation of SCAP directives SCAPIN 233 dated
3 November 1945 and SCAPIN 1154 dated 24 August 1946, it would
appear that the provisions of Imperial Ordinance No. 31l could be
invoked.
4. The Japanese Bureau of Fisheries was notified by this
Section that the provisions for penalties for violations of

=== Page 526 === (confidence 0.99)
whaling regulations were inadequate in comparison to those
provided by the United States which has a maximum penalty of
$10,000 or 6 months imprisonment or both. In order to clarify
the penalty provisions and to increase the penalties the Bureau
has instigated proceedings to have a new Imperial Ordinance issued
which will deal with penalties for violations of whaling regulations
exclusively.
5. A memorandum to the Imperial Japanese Government is being
prepared requesting that biological investigations be undertaken
and the specific data requested in the subject radio are noted.
Issuance of this memorandum is being delayed until the procedures
for biologists as mentioned in the last paragraph of the subject
radio is received from Washington.
6. Data on the type and kind of flag to be used by the
Japanese Antarctic whaling fleet will be the subject of a separate
check-sheet to you.
7. According to statistical records submitted to Natural
Resources Section, SCAP, by the Japanese Bureau of Fisheries, no
right whales were killed during 1945-1946 Bonin whaling operations.
3 Inc1s:
Incl 1 - Regulation Law of Factory Whaling. (In Dupl)
Incl 2 - Ministrial Ordinance. (In Dupl)
Incl 3 - Extracts of the Articles of Penal Provisions in the
Japanese Fishery Law. (In Dupl)
- - - H.G.S. -

My dear Judge:
Don't ever get yourself in a position where you promise a short letter giving a recital
of an extensive transaction. It's tough.
I am enclosing the letter we were discussing about the matter of agar to Mr. Patterson,
the Secretary of War. I am assuming he is the person to be addressed. I know you wili
guide me as to whether he is the one or not.
Frankly, Judge, I am not sure that I have presented the matter clearly or that it con-
tains everything it should. You try to condense things and you lose perspective, so I
don't know just what kind of a job I have done. Our hope lies in the fact that it will
have your support.
I am here to follow any instructions and suggestions you give me, and I shall be more
than grateful to receive them. I am taking the liberty of sending a copy of this to
Charlie Jackson, who was present, of course, during our discussions.
I need not reiterate what a pleasure it has been to me to have met you both in Chicago
and in Virginia Beach. You are one of the few men that fits all of the expectations.
My kindest regards to you and Mrs. Bland.
Sincerely,

October 4th, 1946
Hon. Robert P. Patterson
Secretary of War
Washington, D. C.
Dear Mr. Secretary:
This company is known as American Agar and Chemical Company. The word
"Chemical" in the name, however, is practically a misnomer, because we handle
no chemicals except agar, which is manufactured by us from a weed knowm as
Gelidium for which diving operations have to be conducted. The weed occurs
in the ocean from 15 to 30 feet under water, and is usually found in rocky
formations. Agar is used in medicine, pharmacy, food and industry. Its
critical use, however, is in laboratories, where it is used for the purpose
of permitting culture growth upon slides, and in which use there is no sub-
stitute for true agar, that is, agar made from Gelidium. Before the war,
Japan had practically a world monopoly on the production of agar.
There have been several attempts in the past to manufacture agar in this
country, but they were unsuccessful because of the difficulty of securing the
weed, of which the main source is Mexico. So far as we know, before the war,
when it was possible to obtain weed in Mexico, the Japanese used to harvest it
or buy it up and ship it to Japan.
My associate,who is the President of the company, became interested in
agar about l937, realizing the necessity for the product and our almost com-
plete dependence upon Japan for our supply of it. By rendering financial
assistance to a gentleman who was attempting to revive the industry again in
California, this company was formed, and during the war we were practically
the only supplier of agar to Defense Supplies Corporation, and various contract
holders of the Army and Navy. Strange as it may seem, the operation was not a
particularly profitable one, due to the difficulty of securing the raw material,
and due to poor enforcement of its regulations by the War Production Board.
We tried to develop a source of raw material in California, spending substantial
amounts of money, but results were very poor. In a further attempt to assure a
permanent supply of the agar weed, that is, Gelidium, we entered into a contract
with a Mexican company, so that we could enable them to comply with a concession
that they had from the Mexican government for the exploitation of the Gelidium.
This necessitated the erection of a plant at Ensenada, Lower California, at our
expense, but in which we have no ownership interest, our sole interest being that,
by the contract, the Mexican company was to supply us with all of the excess

=== Page 530 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
Gelidium which they produce beyond their own needs. We are keeping that avenue
of supply open, though it has become rather meager by reason of the fact that
the Mexican producers have so many more profitable items available from time to
time, particularly insofar as divers are concerned, abalone at the present time
offering fabulous earnings.
If the Mexican source of supply had to be abandoned, for lack of raw mate-
rial, in the event of another war we again would be dependent entirely for
supplies of agar upon Japan, as the quantities of the raw material obtainable
along the California coast are negligible.
It would appear that it would be well to have an American agar industry that
can not only relieve the present world wide demand for agar, but can be ready to
supply the Government and its contractors with agar in the event of any future
necessity. To maintain that industry it has to be supplied with adequate raw
materials so that it can exist during competitive times.
There is a lot of Gelidium in Japan and also around the possessions that
she has lost, such as Formosa, Korea, and Karafuto, and if we were able to
secure sufficient quantities of the weed from Japan, we could keep the industry
going in this country, and, at the same time, preserve the Mexican source so
that it would be available in case of an emergency where critical needs would
have to be supplied from close-by sources. Moreover, the sale of the weed to
us from Japan, in addition to keeping the industry alive in this country and
supplying this country with very high-grade agar, would also provide an outlet
for raw materials for Japan, because the weed has been cultivated around those
islands for, so far as we know, centuries. If the disposal of any raw material
also is a reparations problem, that would be aided thereby. The raw material
would be paid for in dollars in this country.
It is not our desire to suppress the Japanese manufacture of agar or kill
a Japanese industry. We do feel, however, that if it is at all possible an
American industry supplying a critical need should be kept alive, without, of
course, any cost to the Government.
of
The maintenance of the industry in this country and the securing/the
supply of raw material, I think would be best arranged if it were possible to go
to Japan and be of any aid possible to the Military Government in making the
necessary arrangements. I would greatly appreciate it if arrangements for a
visit of short duration could be made, so that the problem could be worked out
there one way or the other.
Naturally, a fuller recital of the matters herein set forth would be less
jarring and more clearly establish the importance of preserving an adequate
agar industry in the United States, but it is for the sake of brevity that this
recital is sketchily written. I need not, however, state that any and all
information will be made available and presented in a more comprehensive form.

=== Page 531 === (confidence 0.99)
-3
What relief we are to receive, however, by way of being able to get
proper supplies, has to be done within a reasonable period of time.
Respectfully submitted
AMERICAN AGAR AND CHEMICAL COMPANY
Benjamin W. Shipman
Vice President

Dear Mr. Secretary:
As Chairman of the Conmittee on Merchant Marine and
Fisheries of the House of Representatives, I am very much interested
in the manufacture of agar by the American Agar and Chemical Company,
of San Viego, Jalifornia, of which Mr. Benjamin N. Shipman is Presi-
dent. I am writing to solicit your personal study of the beneficial,
medicinal, and laboratory uses that are made of true agar, - that is,
agar made from the weed, known as Gelidium.
It appears that at one time we were solely dependent upon
Japan for agar, but that durin: the war the american Agar and Chem-
ical Company was the onl: source of supply of this product to
Defense Suprlies Corporation and various contract holders of the
Army and Navy.
It anpears that the need for a rermanent supply of the agar
weed necessitated a contrict by the American Company with a Mexican
Connany in order to secure the benefit of certain concessions made
by the Mexican Government to the Mexican Company, and this necessi-
tated the erection of a plant at Ensenade, Lower California, at the
expense of the Americn Company, but in which it had no ownership
interest further than that the rexican Conpany was to supply all
of the excess Gelidium projuced by them beyond their own needs.
The Mexican producers have many more profitable items available
from time to time so far as their divers are concerned, as for in-
stance, abalone at the present time offers fabulous returns.
There is an ample sup ly of Gelidium in Japan, around her
former possessions of Formosa, Korea, and narafuto. If these sup-
plies could be made available to the merican Company, it would
enable them to survive and also to preserve the Lexican source so
that it vould be available in cuse of an emergency. The preserva-
tion and maintenance of an American Company is essential, and I am
told that this may be done without cost to the United States if
properly encouraged. The sale for Japan would be paid for in
dollars in this country, and contribute to the promotion of commerce
in the United States and prove a source of profit for Japan.

=== Page 533 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
If, therefore, a plan can be worked out whereby the
Japanese supply of Gelidiem may be used by the American Company,
it will contribute to promote the trade with Mexico and Japan, and
to preserve an essential American industry.
I know Mr. Shipman, President of the American Company.
He is reliable, and I believe the matter sufficiently important
for consideration by the Secretaries of War, Interior, and Commerce.
accordingly, I am writing similarly to each Secretary indicated
with the hope that a united effort may be made to solve this problem
in a way to promote the interest of America, Mexico and Japan.
I hope that Mr. Shipman may be afforded an opportunity,
if he so desires, of submitting in greater detail the factors
incidental to the development of this industry and the maintenance
of the existing industry.
Yours very sincerely,
Chairman

liy dear ir. Carver:
Thank you for your kind letter of October 17, 1946 with
reference to the Antarctic catch limitation on whales and to
Japanese whaling.
The officials of the Federal Government are in full agree-
ment with your opinion on the Antarctic catch limitation, namely
that a limit on catoh should be maintained. I agree with you
that from a strictly conservation standpoint, the figure of 16,000
units is too high to assure rebuilding of the stocks. At the same
time, there will undoubtedly be pressure for an increase to 20,000
units from certain delegations because the fats and oils outlook
for 1947-48, as well as the outlook for whale oil prices, is not
yet clear.
With respect to the Japanese whaling operations, the Norwegian,
British, New Zealand, Soviet and Australian Governments have been
given full explanations of General kacarthur's action along the
CS/V
following lines:
1. This authorization is on a one-season basis and there is
no commitment on the part of the United States to agree to future
expeditions.
2. Determination of the future of Japanese whaling, as well as
the disposition of Japanese vessels, completed or under construction,
is a matter for allied determination.

It is understood that this will be an allied expedition under
the occupation flag and that inspection will be undertaken as an allied
operation. It is further understood that the catch of the expeditien
